# The Indian Money Book: Everything About Personal Finance, Money, Law, and Freedom

Written for India, built for 2026.

This book is a complete, practical guide to personal finance for Indian readers, covering everything from your first salary to your final retirement, from legal savings to grey-zone tax planning to outright illegal traps you must avoid. It is written to be read on paper, studied slowly, or played through text to speech from start to finish. Every chapter is in plain, spoken style, with no tables, no jargon left unexplained, and every rupee figure grounded in the Indian reality.

## How to Use This Book

If you are new to money, start at the beginning and go in order. The first part gives you the foundations: how to think, how to budget, how to build a safety net, and how to understand your credit. The middle parts are where your wealth is made: how to earn more, how to invest, and how to use every government scheme and every asset class correctly. The later parts are where you protect everything you built: insurance, retirement, the tax system, and the legal documents that keep your family safe when you are gone. The final parts are the dark map: the scams, the grey zones, the illegal edges, and the family money conversations no one tells you to have.

If you are already experienced, use the table of contents to jump to the chapter that matches your current problem, and then read the three chapters around it, because money problems almost never live in just one place.

## A Word Before You Begin

This book is educational and written for a broad audience. It is not legal, tax, or investment advice for your specific situation. The numbers, interest rates, tax slabs, and rules quoted here reflect the position in the financial year 2025 to 26 and the year 2026, and they change with every budget, every bank board meeting, and every court judgment. Before you make any large decision, a purchase, a tax filing, a legal document, or an investment, check the latest official figures and talk to a qualified chartered accountant, a licensed financial advisor, or a lawyer, because the specific facts of your life will decide what is right for you.

Nothing in this book, including the sections on the legal and grey zones, is an instruction to break any law. Those chapters exist so you understand the lines, so you can recognise when someone is pushing you across one, and so you can make decisions with open eyes. You are responsible for your own choices and for keeping them on the right side of the law.

## What Is Inside, At a Glance

The book is organised into seven parts and forty chapters.

Part One, Chapters 1 to 5, covers Foundations: why most Indians get money wrong, your budget and income, the emergency fund, credit and your credit score, and the psychology of money.

Part Two, Chapters 6 to 12, covers Earning and Borrowing: salary negotiation, entrepreneurship and side income, every type of Indian loan, when borrowing is right, the dark side of lending, money and marriage, and the full map of Indian money instruments.

Part Three, Chapters 13 to 20, covers Investing: mutual funds, index funds, direct stocks, bonds and the fixed income ladder, the government pension stack of PPF, EPF, and NPS, real estate, gold and fixed deposits, and the 2026 frontier of crypto and alternative assets.

Part Four, Chapters 21 to 24, covers Tax: the full picture of Indian income tax, legal deductions and exemptions, capital gains and the tax on your money making money, and how to file your return and deal with the department.

Part Five, Chapters 25 to 31, covers Protection and Retirement: health insurance, life insurance, estate planning, planning for your parents, retirement and the financial independence number, and the social security safety net.

Part Six, Chapters 32 to 36, covers the Law and the Grey Zone: every common scam, the legal strategies that are not illegal and the ones that are, the truly illegal zone, the legal documents every Indian should have, and the family money dynamics everyone ignores.

Part Seven, Chapters 37 to 40, covers the 2026 landscape, your 90 day action plan, the 5 year money roadmap for three income levels, and the money mindset for the rest of your life.

## Part One: Foundations

## Chapter 1: Why Most Indians Get Money Wrong

There is a strange thing about money in our country. India has more people than almost anywhere on Earth, and more families quietly building wealth than anyone in our history ever did. And yet, if you ask the average working adult one simple question, "do you feel financially secure?", the honest answer is usually a quiet "no." It is not because most of us are poor. It is not even because we earn too little. It is because we were never taught a single proper lesson about money, and we filled that gap with guesswork, fear, and the opinions of relatives who are not doing much better than us.

This book closes that gap. It is not written for chartered accountants or traders. It is written for you: the engineer in Hyderabad, the teacher in Indore, the doctor in Kolkata, the professional in Bengaluru, the manager in Pune who feels like the money just disappears every month. If you have ever opened your bank statement and thought, "I have no idea where this money went," this book is for you.

### The Gap Between What We Think and What Actually Works

A friend at a wedding gets a new job and earns four times what you do. He says he is going into the stock market because "everyone is getting rich in stocks." You say he should buy gold, because "gold never loses value." Your neighbor says he should keep his salary in the bank, because "the bank is the only safe place." All three are sincere. All three are guessing. None has run a single number.

That is the gap this book closes. Most Indians hold strong opinions about money, but those opinions were assembled from three sources: family stories, what you saw your parents do, and the one piece of advice a well meaning relative gave at a family lunch. The result is a patchwork of beliefs that contradict each other. We buy insurance only after a friend dies. We park our savings in fixed deposits because our mothers kept them there, while inflation quietly eats their real value year after year. We avoid the stock market because it feels like gambling, and then wonder why our savings do not grow fast enough to cover the school fees, the loan, the wedding, and the retirement all at once.

What actually works is boring, systematic, and built on a small set of habits: knowing your income and expenses to the last rupee, building a cushion before you do anything exciting, spending less than you earn by design, and putting every extra rupee to work where it will compound for twenty or thirty years. That is not a trick. That is a structure. This book is that structure. It will not make you rich by next Diwali. It will give you a life where money stops being a source of dread and starts being a tool you control.

### The Seven Biggest Money Myths in India

Before we build anything, we have to clear the ground. Here are the seven myths that quietly mislead most Indian households.

1. Myth one: "I am too young, or I earn too little, to need a plan." This is the most dangerous myth of all, because time is the one ingredient in money that you cannot buy back. A twenty five year old who puts two thousand rupees a month into a low cost index fund and lets it run for forty years ends up with roughly ten crore rupees or more, and most of that figure was not the money that went in; it was the interest on the interest. If you wait until forty, you end up with about a quarter of what the early starter did. The myth feels comforting. It is expensive.

2. Myth two: "Gold is the safest place to park my money." Gold is a safe place to park value, which is a different thing. Over long stretches, gold has beaten inflation, which is genuinely useful. But it does not pay you anything, it does not compound, and buying a new set of gold on every wedding and every festival has, for most families, turned gold into a storage bin rather than an investment. We will look at gold later.

3. Myth three: "The bank is the only safe place for my money." The bank is safe for keeping your money, not for growing it. A savings account today typically pays two to three percent a year, while prices in the major Indian cities rise faster than that. The bank is a parking space. It is not the car.

4. Myth four: "Only rich people need a financial plan." The exact opposite is true. A professional on a lakh a month who spends ninety eight thousand of it has no cushion, no plan, and no time to invest. That person is far more vulnerable to a job loss than the shopkeeper on a modest income who keeps every rupee he can. Planning matters most for people who cannot afford to lose their money, which is to say, people like us.

5. Myth five: "The stock market is gambling." Gambling is a bet with no structure and no time horizon. Buying shares of good companies on a fixed schedule, over decades, while ignoring the daily noise, is a completely different activity. The confusion is understandable: the headlines are designed to make markets look like casinos. We will untangle this later.

6. Myth six: "Insurance is an investment." When a broker tells you that your life insurance policy is a great investment, treat it as a signal to walk out of the room. Insurance is a tool that protects a family from the shock of losing a bread winner or from a large medical bill. It is not a wealth building tool, and treating it that way is how most Indian households end up with too much paper and not enough money.

7. Myth seven: "I will start when my income goes up." This is myth one wearing a suit. The habit of saving and investing on a fixed schedule is a skill you build by doing it at small amounts. A person who saves five hundred rupees a month without missing a single one for three years is infinitely closer to financial freedom than a person who saves nothing and is promised they will start next year. Start small, start now, and the amounts will grow with your income naturally.

None of these myths are about intelligence. They are all about missing information and missing structure, which means they can all be fixed.

### What This Book Will Do for You

This is a large book, divided into parts, and each part is divided into chapters, so you can read it straight through like a story, or jump around like a manual. Here is the layout.

Part one, the part you are in now, is about foundations: understanding your relationship with money, tracking where it goes, building your emergency cushion, understanding your credit score, and learning why your own brain is the single biggest risk to your wealth. Everything later depends on this part.

Part two is about protection: insurance, in every kind, explained without jargon, so that one bad day does not wipe out twenty years of savings.

Part three is about building: the difference between a mutual fund and an exchange traded fund, how a systematic investment plan actually compounds, and how to pick a handful of boring, low cost vehicles that can carry you from a monthly salary to financial independence. We will treat the stock market the way it deserves to be treated, as a machine you feed, not a casino you visit.

Part four is about life events: buying a home, funding a child's education, wedding costs, and the tax rules behind every one of them.

Part five is about the long game: retirement, the mathematics of stopping work without running out of money, and what happens to your money when you pass away, which is a topic almost no Indian family talks about until it is far too late.

And running through the whole book is one single idea: every chapter ends with a small, doable, thirty minute action. This book is not meant to impress you. It is meant to make you do one small thing this month, and then the same thing next month, for the next ten years.

### The Five Money Personalities

Here is a tool you will keep using through the whole book. Every person is dominated by one of five money temperaments. None of the five is bad. A plan that ignores them fails; a plan that uses them wins.

1. The Saver. The saver looks at every rupee and asks, "will I still need this?" A good saver lives below their means and never runs out of money. The risk is that the saver can become so focused on keeping money that they forget to make it grow. Saving without investing is a leaky bucket.

2. The Spender. The spender is not careless, usually. They genuinely enjoy the life the money makes possible: the trip, the good dinner, the phone upgrade. A good spender knows exactly what they are buying and can name a reason. The risk is the unexamined spend, the one that feels like nothing on its own but adds up to a vanishing salary by the last week of the month.

3. The Investor. The investor is already in the market, already reading the numbers, already tracking the portfolio. The risk is that the investor becomes the portfolio: their identity gets fused with a stock price, and they start making life decisions based on what a fund did this week.

4. The Earner. The earner solves money problems by earning more: a bigger job, more projects, a side hustle. The risk is the treadmill, where a raise is spent on a bigger car before the first salary credit has even hit the account.

5. The Protector. The protector is the person in the family who thinks about what could go wrong. The one who makes sure everyone has insurance, who makes the will, who plans for the worst so the family can live in the best. The risk is spending so much time worrying about the downside that the upside never gets built.

You are probably not one of these. You are probably a two or three combination, and it changes with your life. The young professional is often earner and spender. The person who just had a child becomes protector. The person who retires becomes saver. Knowing which combination you are in today is the first act of money maturity: every plan in this book works better when it is matched to the person, not the other way around.

### Your Three Minute Money Self Audit

Before you close this chapter, do one small exercise that takes three minutes. Do it in your head, or write the answers on paper. Be honest with yourself, not with anyone else. This audit does not grade you. It takes a picture.

1. In the last month, can you name, to within five hundred rupees, how much you earned and how much you spent? Not a rough "probably a little more than I made." An actual number you could say out loud. If you cannot, that is your first project, and it is the whole of this part.

2. How many months could you keep living the way you are actually living, without working a single day? If the answer is under one, you have a job to do, and Chapter 3 is your homework. If it is more than six, you are ahead of a lot of people, and the next part of the book is where you put that advantage to work.

3. On a scale from one to ten, how calm do you feel on the first day of the month, when the salary hits? Ten is "money is just a number that moves around." One is "the first day of the month makes me anxious, because I know what it has to cover by the fifteenth." Most people land between two and six. That is not a verdict. That is a place to start.

4. What is the single biggest money worry in your life right now? Write it down in one sentence. The interesting part is that it is almost never the biggest money problem. It is usually the worry that has been talked about the most at home, the one your mind has been rehearsing. We will come back to that sentence in Chapter 5.

5. Which of the five money personalities is your dominant one this year? Which two are the others? Do not rush. You get to look at yourself here in a way you probably have not done before.

That is the whole audit. Three minutes. No app, no one else in the family involved. Most of us get money wrong not because we are careless, but because we have never been handed the structure this book gives you. Now you have the structure. The next chapter is where you build the floor everything else will stand on.

## Chapter 2: Your Money Foundations: Budget, Income, and Expenses

Here is a hard truth that sounds unkind but is the most liberating idea in this book. If you cannot say how much money you have and where it is going, you do not have a money problem, you have a money mystery, and a mystery is something you can never finish. No investing strategy, no tax plan, no clever trick will work for a person who does not know their own numbers.

This chapter is a slow, careful one. We will read your own pay, build a budget that fits a real Indian salary, understand the invisible leak of lifestyle inflation, and learn two budgeting methods that work even when your family keeps no records.

### How to Read Your Own PFO, or Payslip

At most Indian companies, the payslip that lands in your inbox at the start of the month is sometimes called the PFO, the Personal Financial Overview, or simply the payslip. Most people open it once, see the number at the bottom, and close the app. That is like getting a medical report and reading only the final line. Here is what you should actually look at, in order.

1. The gross, which is the total before anything is taken out. This is the number your boss quotes and the number the new office chat group asks for. It is not the number that ever reaches your bank account.

2. The deductions, the pieces that go out before the money reaches you. These usually include your portion of the provident fund, and the matching portion the company adds, the professional tax in some states, and, in some companies, a contribution to group insurance.

3. The tax that comes out under the income tax rules, which depends on which regime you have chosen under the income tax laws of India: the older regime with its list of deductions, or the newer, simpler one that gives you fewer ways to reduce taxable income. This number changes a lot from year to year, and we will handle it in detail in Part four.

4. The net, which is the number that actually lands in your bank account every single month. This is your real money. This is the only number you will use for the budget in this chapter, and I want you to write it down somewhere you will see it.

A short example, so the words become concrete. Say your gross is sixty thousand rupees a month. The provident fund takes out its share, tax takes a further slice, and maybe the company deducts a little for group benefits. At the end, the number that hits your bank might be around forty two thousand. That forty two thousand is your world for the next thirty days. Every plan in this chapter is built on that bottom line, not on the sixty.

One more thing to check on the payslip, every year, when the new financial year begins in April. Confirm that the provident fund and the tax deductions have been updated to your real salary, and that the company has not silently kept last year's numbers. It happens more than people think, and it costs you a few thousand rupees of untaxed savings every year.

### The 50 30 20 Rule, Adapted for a Real Indian Salary

You have probably heard of the 50 30 20 rule: spend fifty percent of your income on needs, thirty percent on wants, and put twenty percent into investing and savings. In practice, most Indian households do not get to 50 30 20, because "needs" in India is not what it is in a textbook. In our lives, needs are also the school fees, the marriage of your younger cousin, the festival gifts, the annual trip to your parents' village, and, for many families, a home loan that is still running.

Here is the version I want you to actually use, adjusted for India. Take your net salary, the forty two thousand from the example, and split it into four piles, not three.

1. The Essentials, around forty to forty five percent of the net. Rent or loan, food, the phone, the transport, the school fees. This is the pile that gets you through the month on autopilot. If this is over forty five percent of your net, you have a problem, and we will talk about it later in this chapter.

2. The Life, around twenty percent. The trips, the good coffee, the phone you have been wanting, the occasional dinner out. This is not guilt. This is the part of the month that makes the work worth doing.

3. The Paying Off, around fifteen to twenty percent. The home loan that has not finished, the car still running on its EMI, EMI standing for Equated Monthly Installment, which is the fixed amount a bank asks you to pay every month to slowly repay a loan. This pile is how you end the past and free the future.

4. The Future, at least ten percent, and more if you can manage it. This is the pile that goes into investments, into the emergency cushion, into the old age account. It is the only pile that, in ten years, will not be a memory, but something you own.

Notice what changed from the textbook version. The Paying Off pile is its own thing, because in India most families are still in the middle of paying for the house they live in, and that is not a want, it is a commitment that was already made. And the Future pile, at ten percent, is smaller than the textbook's twenty, because in our context it is genuinely hard to reach twenty without cutting so much that you cannot sustain the plan. Ten percent, kept every single month, is the version of this rule that most real Indian households can keep without hating themselves by week two.

### The Real Cost of Lifestyle Inflation in India

Lifestyle inflation is the quiet tax we pay when our income rises and our spending rises with it, faster than the income. It does not hurt the day it happens, which is why it is so easy to let slide.

Here is the Indian version of it. You get a promotion, and your salary moves from one lakh a month to one and a half. The first month feels wonderful. The second month, the cab service you were rationing is now available whenever you want, and you use it. The third month, the apartment in a better neighborhood no longer feels like a sacrifice, so you move, and the rent goes up. By the end of the year, the extra fifty thousand is mostly gone. What is left of it is a bigger phone, a car a grade up, and a flatter, more convenient flat. All of it is fine. All of it is also the reason that after five years on a bigger salary, you have not moved any closer to the number that would let you stop working.

The fix is not to stop enjoying the raise. It is to name a portion of the new money, before it arrives, and put it out of reach of the new lifestyle. Say you decide twenty thousand of the new fifty will go into an index fund on a fixed schedule, and the other thirty quietly raises your living standards, all in moderation. This one habit, deciding what the new money is for before it gets mixed in, is the difference between a raise that compounds for decades and a raise that evaporates in the quarter it arrives.

### Your Monthly Cash Flow Statement, in Plain Words

You do not need to know the phrase "cash flow statement" to make one. You need one sheet of paper, or one app you trust, and one habit that takes ten minutes at the end of every month. The statement answers exactly three questions.

1. What did I have at the start of the month? That is your savings at the beginning of the period, plus anything you are expecting to earn, including your net salary and any side income.

2. What did I send out? Rent, food, transport, EMIs, school, phone, subscriptions, the festival, the cousin's wedding, the trip home.

3. What was left, and where did it go? That is the gap between what came in and what went out. If the gap is zero, you are exactly where you think you are, which is more than most households can say. If the gap is negative, you have a leak, and we will find it.

Here is how to actually build the statement, without a spreadsheet, without a formula. At the end of the month, open your bank app and your card app. Read the list of debits for the month, and group them into three piles in your head: the things you will have again next month, the things that are one time, and the things you forgot you were paying for. The "forgot" pile is where the real money is. Almost every Indian household I have seen has at least one to two thousand rupees a month of forgotten money in that pile: a subscription nobody watches, a free trial that turned into a paid one, an auto pay that quietly doubled in price. When you write this down once, on a real list, the habit becomes permanent, because the version of you who writes it down gets, on average, one to two thousand rupees back every month.

### The Envelope Method and Zero Based Budgeting

Two old, simple, almost unglamorous tools, and both work better than most people expect.

The envelope method is what it sounds like. You divide the next month's money into physical envelopes, or into different accounts if you are more digital than paper: one for food, one for transport, one for bills, one for the life, one for the future. When an envelope is empty, you do not spend from it. You wait, you plan, or you move money from another envelope that has a surplus. The version that actually works for a modern Indian household is the digital one: one savings account with separate reserved balances for each pile, or, if you have a partner, one account with agreed rules. The hardest part of the envelope method is not the money. It is the agreement between two people about whose envelope is whose.

Zero based budgeting is a slightly different tool. In a zero based budget, every rupee that came in has a job before the month begins. The food gets its share, the bills get theirs, the future gets its share, and the total of the jobs equals the total of the money, so that by the end of the month the difference is zero. Nothing is left unassigned and slowly drifting into impulse. The name is not a joke. The point is that no money is left without a decision. A rupee with no decision is not a rupee. It is a small accident waiting for a place to happen.

Both tools have a cost, and I want to be honest about it. They take a few minutes every month, and for a household that does not do this today, the first month is painful, because you will discover money that has been quietly leaking for years. Do not skip the first month because it is painful. That is exactly the month you need it.

### The Common Indian Money Leaks

Before I send you off to do the first month's statement, I want to name the leaks that show up in almost every Indian household we meet, so that when you see them in your own statement, you do not wonder whether they are "normal." None of them are wrong. All of them add up.

1. The EMIs that were not your choice. The home loan, the car loan, sometimes the education loan, all running at once, and all of them taking more than a third of your net. If your EMIs are over forty percent of your net salary, the money for the Future pile is going to be very hard to find, and you should start the zero based budget this month, not next.

2. The social obligation. The cousin's wedding, the friend's baby, the colleague's new job. Each one is a modest amount, but the calendar of events in one Indian family over a year can quietly run to fifty to seventy five thousand, and the money goes out before the month is over. The fix is not to refuse the invites. It is to put the event into the zero based budget at the start of the month, so that when the envelope is opened, the money is already set aside, not raided from the food pile.

3. The festival that costs more than the festival. Diwali, and the gifts, and the new clothes, and the sweets, and the trip home. For most households, the real cost of the two biggest festivals is anywhere from ten to twenty thousand, and it arrives with no warning because it was never in the budget. A single line in the statement, "festival fund, ten thousand," fixes it, and the festival still happens. It just stops being a loan.

4. The app, the subscription, the free trial. Small, almost invisible, and they do not get cut on their own, because nobody is cutting them. The ten minute monthly review that we built earlier in this chapter is the only thing that finds them.

5. The trip home that is not just the trip home. The train ticket, the stay, the money you give to your parents, the cousins' kids, the village wedding you had to attend. It is the one that hits every household every year, and the one nobody budgets for, because budgeting for family in our culture feels like it should be automatic. Automatic is the problem. Automatic things do not get a line in the statement, and things without a line in the statement are the things that eat the Future pile.

That is the whole of the foundation. You can now read your payslip, build a budget that matches a real Indian salary, understand what lifestyle inflation is and how to stop it, write a cash flow statement in ten minutes, use two budgeting methods that do not require a degree, and recognize the five leaks that show up in every household. The next chapter is where we put some of that monthly money to the job it has been waiting for.

## Chapter 3: The Emergency Fund: Your First Real Goal

I want to start this chapter with a story that is not a story, because it happens to so many people in this country that it has become its own kind of folklore. A professional in Bengaluru, earning a good salary, keeping the savings in a fixed deposit because the family always did. A hospital emergency hits the family. Not the person; a parent, far away. The money that comes into the account is not the person's own. It is a loan from a relative, at a rate that is never spoken about. The emergency is handled, the loan is paid back, and the household is now quietly behind on its money goals by a year or two, because the fixed deposit had to be broken, and breaking it early means losing the interest and, in some cases, paying a penalty.

This is not a story about luck. This is a story about not having the emergency fund. It is not glamorous. It is not going to make you rich. It is going to make you the person who, when the bad day comes, does not have to sell something, borrow from a cousin, or quietly let a goal slip by a year. That is worth more than any investing trick in this book, because it is the only one that protects you from the one event that can undo everything.

### Why Six to Twelve Months, and How to Calculate Yours

The number you will hear everywhere is six months. Six months of expenses, sitting somewhere safe, touching the ground only when the ground touches you first. For a lot of households in India, six is too few, and here is why.

If you are salaried, your income is not just your salary. It is your salary, plus the annual bonus, plus the festival money from family, plus the occasional side gig. Six months calculated on the salary alone is not the real six months. The real number, for a salaried professional, is closer to eight or ten, because when the salary is the only thing you count on, the bonus and the side income quietly disappear from the plan, and the plan becomes too small to hold the family through a real shock.

If you are self employed, running a business or a clinic or a shop, the honest number is twelve. Not because twelve is magic, but because when the income itself is the thing that can stop, the cushion has to be long enough to hold you through a slow quarter, not just a bad week. A lot of small business owners in India will tell you that the quarter that does not come back is the one they had not planned for.

So the calculation, in one clean sentence: take your monthly essentials, the rent or the loan, the food, the school, the bills, the transport, the insurance premiums, and multiply by the number of months that fits your situation. Six if you are salaried and the family is stable. Eight to ten if you have a bonus and a side income that you are not sure will keep arriving. Twelve if the income itself is the uncertain thing. That number, in rupees, is the size of the fund you are building. It is not a goal for next year. It is a target for the next two to three years, and it gets paid for with a fixed amount out of the Future pile, which is why the Future pile in Chapter 2 is the only pile that builds the cushion.

### Where to Keep It: Liquid Funds versus a Savings Account versus Digital Gold

The fund has to do two jobs. It has to be there when you need it, which means it has to be reachable within a day or less. And it has to keep up with the cost of living, which means it has to earn more than the bank gives you.

Here is how the three options stack up.

1. A savings account, the most common place, and the most common mistake. The account is there, it is reachable, it is easy, and it pays two to three percent a year, which is the same number that inflation in the big Indian cities has been quietly beating for the last few years, on average. In other words, the money is not losing. It is just not keeping up. A savings account is not a place to park the fund. It is a place to park the money that is moving through the month, on its way to a bill or the market.

2. A liquid fund, which is a type of mutual fund that keeps the money in very short term, very safe debt. It earns a bit more than the bank, around five to six percent, and it is reachable within a day, often within a few hours. The fund is still conservative, it is not a stock fund, and it is not going to make you rich, which is the whole point. For most households in India, the liquid fund is the right home for the emergency fund, with one small rule: keep the first one or two months of essentials in the savings account for the truly immediate emergencies, and keep the rest of the fund in the liquid fund, for the emergencies that arrive on a timescale of a few days.

3. Digital gold, the one that sounds like a good idea and is not one. You have seen the ads, and the argument is that gold is safe, gold is liquid, and you can buy and sell it on your phone. In practice, for an emergency fund, digital gold fails both jobs. It is not safe, because the price of gold moves, and the day you need it most is not necessarily the day it is at a high price. It is not as liquid as it looks, because selling a small weight on a busy evening is not the same as withdrawing from a bank account in ten seconds. Gold has a real place in a family's money, and we will come back to it later in the book. It is just not the right one for the emergency fund.

A short note on the fixed deposit, which a lot of families use. A fixed deposit is fine for money you do not need for a specific period. It is not the right home for the emergency. The moment you break it, you lose the interest you were promised and, in many cases, pay a penalty, and the penalty is the last thing you want on the one day you need to reach your money fast.

### How Much for a Tier One City versus a Tier Two City

Here is where I want to put some real numbers on the table, so the abstract "six to twelve months" starts to look like a target you can point at.

For a household in a Tier one city, say a professional in Bengaluru or a manager in Mumbai, whose monthly essentials are around sixty thousand rupees, the fund is between two point six lakh and five lakh, depending on whether you land at six months or twelve. For a salaried, stable household, two point six lakh is the floor, and four to five lakh is where you want to be by the time you have finished this part of the book. The fund is not a number you check once. It rises with the essentials, because the essentials rise every year, and the fund has to rise with them or it is quietly getting smaller.

For a household in a Tier two city, say a professional in Coimbatore or a teacher in Indore, whose monthly essentials are around twenty five thousand, the fund is between one point five lakh and three lakh. The absolute number is smaller, and the fund is easier to reach in one to two years. That is the reason a lot of families in smaller cities get ahead on this part faster than families in the big cities, not because they earn more, but because the essentials pile is smaller and the gap to the target is shorter.

One thing I want to flag for both, and I mean both, not just the big city households. The fund is not the retirement fund, and it is not the education fund. It is its own account, with its own number, and the money in it stays there, untouched, until the one thing that hits. The temptation, for every household that reaches the target, is to quietly take some of it out for the next car, or the next wedding, or the next upgrade. The moment that happens, the fund is no longer a fund. It is just the next thing you are going to spend. Give the account a label, and let the label be a promise to the version of you who is going to need the money on the bad day.

### When to Use It, and When Not to

The emergency fund is for emergencies. This is not a joke, because the two most common ways the fund gets broken are not emergencies at all.

Use it when: you lose your job and there is no income and the family still has to eat. A parent or a partner gets sick, and the hospital bill is bigger than the day to day money can cover. A disaster hits the home, the roof, the water, the one thing that costs a lot to fix and cannot be put off. A legal situation that needs money now, the one that is not going to wait for the next month's salary.

Do not use it when: a better phone comes out and you want the upgrade. A wedding is coming, and you would like the best outfit, and the fund is the only account with a number that looks big enough. You have a bad month and the food did not come, and the fund is right there, and the easiest thing is to just use some. That last case is the most dangerous, because the fund is doing its job: it is there, it is reachable, and the very fact that it is easy to reach is the reason it gets broken. Once it is broken, the next emergency finds an empty account, and the family goes back to the cousin. Which is exactly the thing the fund was built to avoid.

The rule that has saved more funds than any rule in this book is a one line test, and I want you to write it somewhere you will see it. If the thing you are about to buy with the fund will still matter in six months, use the regular money. If it will not matter in six months, use the regular money. And if you really need the thing, split it into small pieces that fit the month. The fund is for the things that show up on a bad day, not the things that show up on Diwali.

### The Sleep at Night Test

There is one more test, and it is not a number, it is a feeling, and I want to end the chapter with it, because it tells you when you have finished, and not before.

Close your eyes and picture the worst plausible day for your family, not the impossible one, the one that could actually happen. The job goes. The parent gets sick. The car breaks down on the highway, and the repair is not small. Now ask yourself the question, and be honest, because this is a question the family will not get a second chance to answer. Can you sleep on that day? Or do you wake up at two in the morning, counting how many days of food and rent are left, because the fund is not there yet?

If the answer is the second one, the fund is not finished, and you keep building. If the answer is the first one, the fund is finished, and you are allowed to move on to the next part of the book, where the money that is not needed for the bad day finally gets to work for the good ones. That is where the Future pile in Chapter 2 stops being a cushion and starts being a machine.

This is the first real goal, and it is the one every other goal in this book stands on. Everything else can wait a year if the fund has to grow for one more quarter. The fund is the floor, and the floor is not a luxury. It is the thing that makes all the other parts of the book actually true.

## Chapter 4: Credit, Credit Score, and Building Your Money Reputation

Here is something that feels strange when you say it out loud, and I want you to sit with it for a moment before we go on. Your credit score is not a number that a bank gives you, like a grade. It is a number that every lender asks for, and it is not about you. It is the short answer to one question that every single lender in the country asks, every time someone walks in to borrow money: will this person pay me back? And the score is not the answer. It is the summary of the answer, the one that gets read in ten seconds by a system, and the one that decides, before you have even sat down, whether you get the loan, at what rate, and how much of it.

This is not a chapter about getting approved. This is a chapter about the fact that your money reputation, which is the name I am giving it in this book, travels with you. It is not built by one good habit. It is built by ten years of small habits. And it is not lost by one bad month. It is lost by the way the bad month gets recorded.

### What CIBIL Actually Is, in Plain English

The company that runs the most common credit scoring system in India is called CIBIL, which stands for Credit Information Bureau (India) Limited. It is a company, not a bank, not the government, that keeps a file on almost every adult in the country who has ever taken a loan or used a credit card. The file is not a secret government file. It is the one the banks use when they are deciding to lend you money, and the number that comes out of it, a three digit score, is what people call the CIBIL score. It runs from three hundred, which is about as low as it gets, to nine hundred, which is about as good as it gets.

The score itself is not a verdict. It is a summary. The whole file is more than the score. It contains the history of every loan you have ever taken, every card, every payment, every time you paid on time, and every time you did not. When a bank wants to know if you are going to pay them back, they do not call your mother. They do not ask your office. They check the file, because it is the only honest thing they get. It is the only thing written down at the time, when the payments were still happening.

One more thing to understand, because it changes how you think about the whole system. The file is not built by you. It is built by every lender you have ever used, every bank, every card, every loan app, and each one reports to the file on a schedule that is not really up for discussion. You cannot stop the reporting, and you cannot pick which parts of your history get written down, because the history is the whole thing, and the whole thing is what gets scored. The only thing you can control is the next payment, and the one after that, and the one after that.

### The Things That Quietly Damage Your Score

Here are the things that cost Indian households their money reputation, and I want you to read them slowly, because almost all of them sound harmless until the number starts moving.

1. The late payment, the one everyone thinks is covered by a buffer. You miss the due date by a day or two, maybe a week, and the card company reports it. And the report stays in the file for a long time. The file does not forgive one late payment. It does not forgive five. And it does not forgive the one from three years ago that you have completely forgotten, because the score is built from the whole history, and that one late payment is still in the mix.

2. The card limit you keep climbing. If the balance on your credit card sits at the top of the limit every month, the file reads that as a person who is always running out of money, and the score drops, even though the card has never been late. The fix is not paying on time. The card is already paid on time. The fix is paying the balance down so it is not sitting at the top of the limit. The limit means nothing to you personally. It means something to the file.

3. The new loans that arrive in a short stretch. The home loan, the car loan, the personal loan, all within a year. The file reads that as a person taking on more debt than the income can hold, and the score drops, even though every loan is being paid on time. The damage is not the debt. It is the speed. And the speed is the part the household cannot control, because the loans do not arrive one by one. They arrive all at once, in a wedding year, in a new home year, and by the time the household notices the score has dropped, the loans are already in the file and the damage is done.

4. The small loan app you thought was not a loan. The file does not know the loan was small. It does not know it was just for the phone. The file knows that a loan was taken, and that it was repaid, or that it was not. And the not repaid one shows up in the score years later, on the day you are trying to buy the home, and the home is not available at the rate you were hoping for, because of a loan app you thought was not a loan.

5. The file that has never been checked. This one catches almost everyone. The file has a habit of quietly carrying errors: a payment that was made on time but marked late, a loan that was closed but still showing as open, a balance that does not match the bank statement. The errors stay until someone looks at them. The someone is you. The looking is free, it takes ten minutes, and it is the only thing that will ever fix an error that nobody else is going to fix.

### How to Build and Repair Your Score, Step by Step

Here is the repair plan, and I want you to read it as a plan, not as a list of suggestions. Repair is not a suggestion. It is a schedule.

1. Get the file, read it, and find the errors. This is step one, and it is not optional. It is the first thing to do when you sit down for this chapter, and it is the only step that will ever save you from errors that nobody else is going to find.

2. Fix the errors, in writing, with the bank, with the card company, with the lender. Do not accept "we will look into it" as an answer, because the answer is a correction, and the correction is the only thing that moves the number. And the number moves slowly, on a schedule that is not the one you get to pick.

3. Pay down the small debts, the personal loans, the card balances, the loan app balances, in the order where the damage is highest, not where the amount is highest. The damage is the thing showing in the file. The amount is the thing you already know. The file is what the banks see, so the file is what gets fixed first.

4. Keep the credit card balance below a third of the limit, every month, and stay there. The score moves up slowly, on the schedule the file follows, not the one you hope for.

5. Do not take on new loans for as long as you can hold. The file is in repair mode, and repair mode is not a time for new debt. A home loan is not a mistake even in repair mode, if it is planned. The new car is. The car is not going to wait. The file has to. The waiting is the repair.

The repair takes time, and I want to be honest about that time, because it is the part nobody tells you, and it is the reason most people give up halfway. If your score is in the bottom part of the range, the repair is not going to be done in a year. It is going to take two to three years, and that is not a failure. That is the repair. And the repair is the only thing that will make the next big loan possible, and the next big loan is the home, and the home is what the repair is all for.

### Credit Cards in India: How to Use Them Without Falling Into Debt

The card is not the enemy, and I want to say that clearly, because the card has a real job, and the job is not the one most people in this country give it. The job of the card is to be a float: a way to hold money for a few weeks before you pay it back. Used on schedule, the float is not a debt. It is a tool. The trouble in our context is that the float gets used for spending, and the spending turns into debt, and the debt shows up in the file.

Here is how to use the card in India without falling into debt. Read it as a rule set, because it is a rule set, not advice.

1. When the bill comes, pay it in full, on the due date. The payment is the whole amount, not the minimum. The minimum is the number the bank wants you to see, because the minimum is the number that keeps the debt alive. The living debt is the one that costs you interest every month, and the interest is the one you will not find in the file but will definitely find in your account.

2. The limit is not your income, and the limit is not what you can spend. The limit is a number the bank decided to give you. Using the whole limit, every month, is what the file reads as running out of money, and running out of money is what shows up in the score.

3. The card is not the emergency fund. It is not the wedding fund. It is not the trip fund. The card is the float, the float gets paid off, paying off is the habit, the habit builds the file, the file builds the score, the score builds the next loan, and the next loan is what the repair was for.

4. The small loan, the one for the phone, the one for the festival, the one you think is not a loan, is a loan. The loan goes in the file. The file is what the bank reads. The bank is what says no. And the no is what the repair was meant to avoid.

### The Five Year Credit Card History Rule

Here is the rule, and I want you to understand it, because it changes how you think about the card. The file keeps your credit card history for five years, from the day the card was opened to the day it was closed. Five years is the window the score looks through, and the window keeps the card in the mix even after the card is closed.

The rule is not "keep the card for five years." The rule is this: the card is in the file for five years, the file is what the bank reads, the bank is what decides the loan, and the decision is what five years of history is meant to support. If you open a card and close it after a year, the card is still in the file for the full five years. And when the score looks through that window, it sees the year the card was closed, and a closed card reads as a person who does not maintain their credit. So the working version of the rule is simple: open cards you will keep, keep the ones you open active with a small, paid in full balance, and close a card only when you have no good reason to keep it, because the reason you keep it is not convenience. It is the file.

### What Happens When Your Score Is Below Seven Hundred

Seven hundred is the number, and it is not a line the bank draws with a pen. It is the number the file reads as "not a safe borrower." Below seven hundred, the loan does not become a no. It becomes a yes, at a rate that is not the one you were hoping for. And that higher rate costs you more, every single month, for the whole life of the loan. A home loan that runs for twenty years at the wrong rate does not feel expensive month by month. It feels like small change. And then, at the end, the small change adds up to lakhs that a score of seven five would have saved you.

So here is what to do if your score is below seven hundred. Do not apply for the loan, the new card, or the higher limit. Applying adds a mark to the file, and the mark keeps the score down, and the low score makes the next loan even more expensive, and that is the spiral the repair exists to avoid. Instead, run the repair plan from earlier in this chapter, give it the two to three years it needs, and hold one rule in your head the whole time: your money reputation is built by the next payment, and the one after that, and the one after that. That is not a metaphor. That is the whole system, working exactly as designed.

## Chapter 5: The Psychology of Money: Ego, Fear, and Social Pressure

Everything in Parts two through four is math, and math is the easy part. A person who can add can understand an index fund, a tax saving instrument, and a retirement plan. What most people in this country cannot do is sit still long enough to run the plan, because the plan competes with something much older than the bank. It competes with the office, the village, the wedding, the WhatsApp group, and the voice that has been speaking since you were ten years old and someone in the family said, "you are earning so much, buy something." This chapter is about that voice. It is the chapter most people need most, because the day the market drops, it is not the math that makes you sell. It is the voice.

### Why We Show Off the Car but Not the Children's Education

Here is the pattern, and I want you to read it without deciding it is about someone else, because it is about a lot of us.

The car gets bought because it is visible. The car has an audience. Every office, every family function, every group photo has somewhere for the car to stand, and the standing is the point. The children's education has no audience at all. No one at the office asks which school your child went to this year. No one posts about the fee payment. The payoff of the education does not show up for fifteen years, and it shows up in your child's career, not in your car park. So the money flows to the thing with the audience, and the other thing gets the leftover, and the leftover is the amount that the month could not explain.

This is not a moral failing. It is how human beings are wired. We evolved to be watched, and a visible purchase pays a social dividend the day it arrives. An invisible one, like the investment, like the school fee, like the insurance premium, pays nothing you can show anyone, and pays the family something only decades later. The brain prefers the dividend it can show. The family pays for that preference.

The fix is not willpower. It is decision order. The education and the investment get paid for at the start of the month, before the visible things, because visible things only get what is left, and left is where they live or die. The second part of the fix is giving the invisible money a ceremony of its own. When you pay the school fee, when the investment credit hits, say it out loud, to your partner, to your child, because the silent money is the money that gets eaten, and the money that gets named gets kept.

### The Social Comparison Trap: Maruti versus BMW, Flat in Gurgaon versus Flat in Pune

The trap is not that you want the better car. Wanting is natural. The trap is what the wanting is measuring. It is not measuring what you need. It is measuring where you stand against a reference group: the office, the family, the city.

The Maruti versus BMW version works like this. You earn the same as your colleagues. They all bought the BMW a year ago. You are driving the Maruti to the same office, and the office sees both cars, and the office talks about both cars, and by the end of the year your Maruti is not a car, it is a confession. So you buy the BMW, and the relief lasts one month, and then the reference group moves up, because the reference group always moves up. It is a race where the finish line is painted on the back of the person ahead of you.

The flat version works the same way, with a city twist. Your flat in Pune is comfortable, quiet, in a good neighborhood, and costs half of what the equivalent address in Gurgaon costs. And it still bothers you, because the city is a signal, and Gurgaon is the signal, and the office group chat knows which city you live in, and the one in Pune is the one that does not get the photo, and the one that does not get the photo is the one the reference group is going to talk about. So people move their whole lives across cities for a postcard, taking a salary that rises in the new city and rising faster in the new city, and the gap stays the same, because the other side's gap stays the same, too.

How to break the trap: name your reference group out loud. Not the office. Not the city. The family you are building, the body you are keeping healthy, the retirement you are protecting. When the car or the flat comes up, ask the one honest question: if no one would ever know, if the office did not exist and the group chat was deleted and the village was gone, would I still want this, in this size, at this price? Most purchases survive that question smaller, which is the size they were always worth.

### The Biases That Wreck Indian Investors

Here are the moments, and I want you to read them as moments, not ideas, because the moments are what you will feel, and the feeling is what the bias is made of.

1. The crypto FOMO, which stands for Fear of Missing Out. Your friend's cousin, two offices away, made ten times his salary on a coin, and the story lands in the group chat on a Tuesday, and by Thursday the whole office is talking about which app to download. The math that gets ignored is that the cousin is one survivor out of a thousand people who lost, and the one who is talking about it is the one who got out at the top, not the nine hundred and ninety nine who are not posting. The counter rule: money you cannot explain to your partner, in one honest sentence, is money you are not allowed to put in, full stop.

2. The Nifty dip panic. The index falls, the news channel says the word "crash," the headlines are red, and your hands go to the sell button, because selling is the only thing you feel like you can do, and doing something feels better than watching. What selling in the dip actually does is turn a paper loss into a real one, and it means you have to buy back, later, at a higher price, which is the exact opposite of the plan you wrote on a calm day. The counter rule: the plan gets written on the calm day, and the calm day gets read on the red day. You do not make decisions in the red. You follow the decision from the calm.

3. The overconfidence after one good year. Your portfolio had a great year, the numbers went up, and you start to feel like you are good at this, which is the one feeling that is guaranteed to be wrong, because one good year has nothing to do with skill. The market was up. Everyone's portfolio was up. The person who bought nothing at all had the same good year, without doing anything, and that is the test that should stay with you: if doing nothing gave you the same result, the result was not yours. The counter rule: after a good year, do not add, review. Read the plan again, and ask which parts you have drifted from, because the good year is the one that makes you drift, and the drift is the one that eats the next year.

### How to Build a Money Identity That Is Your Own

Here is the tool that ties this whole chapter together, and it is a one sentence tool, but it takes a year to install.

Write down one sentence that says who you are with money, not what you own with it. Not "I drive a BMW." Not "I have a flat in Gurgaon." Those are possessions, and possessions are rented feelings, because the moment the possession goes, the feeling goes, and the rent is forever. The identity sentence is about the behavior, and it goes something like this: "I am the person who pays the plan first and shows the rest to the office." Or, "I am the person who decides on calm days and does not act on red days." Or, "I am the person who names the money at home before it gets eaten."

The reason the identity works where the rule fails is that the rule competes with your mood, and the mood wins sometimes. The identity does not compete with the mood. The mood is not you, the identity is you, and a mood that says "buy the phone" against an identity that says "pay the plan first" has to wait, because you cannot be both at once, and the one you are, is the one you wrote down, in ink, on a calm day.

And install it with the family, not alone, because in an Indian household the identity is not a personal thing. Your parents' expectations of you and your partner's expectations of you are the two strongest forces that will try to overwrite the sentence you wrote. Sit down with your partner, not at the phone, not after the argument, and say the sentence out loud, and let them say their version of it, and write both down. The plan that two people have said out loud survives the festival. The plan that one person is holding inside survives the wedding, which is longer.

### The 48 Hour Rule and the "Will It Still Matter in Six Months" Test

The last tool, and the one you will use the most. The 48 hour rule is this: any purchase that was not in the plan, that arrived out of nowhere, the phone, the trip, the outfit, the upgrade, gets written down with a date, and it does not get bought for two full days. Not a night. Not a morning. Two days, because the wanting has a life span, and the life span of most wantings is shorter than a weekend.

What the two days do is not make you disciplined. They make you accurate. On day one, the wanting is full strength, and you cannot tell what you want from what the office wants, and what the group chat wants, and what the voice in your head that has been speaking since you were ten wants. On day three, the wanting has met the rest of your life, and it is either still there, and smaller, and you know it is a real want, or it is gone, and you have just saved the amount, which is the future pile, which is the machine, which is the whole book.

The second test, for the big ones, is the six months question, and it is the same question from the money self audit in Chapter 1, turned into a gate. Will this still matter in six months? The wedding outfit will not. The car that is going to be underfunded for twenty years will. The emergency fund is already covered, because that is the fund, not a purchase, and the two are not the same thing, and the not same thing is the one the gate catches.

And the same two days apply to the money that is not going out, and this is the part most people miss. Do not make the big money decisions in the red. Not when the index has dropped and your hands are shaking. Not after the argument with the partner, when the whole house feels like a threat. Not after the good year, when the overconfidence is the loudest voice in the room. The 48 hours for decisions is the 48 hours for purchases, wearing a suit, and the suit is what makes it feel like a rule, and the rule is what makes it feel like a habit, and the habit is what makes the identity real.

That is the whole of Part one. You now have the structure, the foundation, the cushion, the reputation, and the mindset that the rest of the book stands on. The next part of the book is where the money that is not needed for the bad day gets to start working for the good ones.

---

## Part Two: Earning More, Borrowing Smarter, and Building Real Wealth

Welcome back. In Part One, we looked at how money moves: how you earn, how you spend, how you build an emergency fund, and how you start investing. This part goes deeper. We are going to talk about how to earn more, because more income is the single biggest engine of wealth for most Indians. Then we will look at borrowing, because a loan is not bad by nature, but how you borrow decides whether it builds you up or breaks you down. We will talk about starting a side business legally, about money inside marriage, and finally we will give you a complete map of every money instrument an Indian person can use. Read or listen slowly. These chapters are long, but each one solves a real problem you will face in the next five years of your life.

## Chapter 6: Earning More, Salary Negotiation in India

Most Indians learn two things about salary at school: save money, and avoid interest. Few learn how to negotiate a higher income, and that is a gap that costs lakhs over a career. Negotiation is not just a senior manager skill. It is a daily adult skill in India, and it compounds every single year you do it well.

### How Indian Salary Bands Actually Work

When an Indian company offers you a job, the number they tell you, called the CTC or cost to company, is not the money that lands in your account. A typical offer sheet breaks down into several pieces. First is the basic salary, which is usually 40 to 60 percent of your gross pay. On top of the basic salary, the company adds allowances such as HRA, which is the house rent allowance, LTA, which is the leave travel allowance, and a special allowance that fills up the remaining difference. Some companies add a medical allowance and a conveyance allowance for commuting. Then there are items that do not show up in your monthly salary at all: the employer contribution to your EPF, which is the Employees Provident Fund, the employer share of your gratuity, insurance, and sometimes equity.

Here is a concrete example. Say a company offers you a CTC of twenty two lakh rupees a year. Your gross salary might be one hundred sixty thousand a month. From that, the employer deducts your own PF contribution, which is 12 percent of your basic pay, your professional tax of a few hundred rupees, and your income tax under the new tax regime. What remains is your take home pay, and it is usually around one hundred fifteen to one hundred twenty five thousand a month, depending on your slab. So of the twenty two lakh the company boasts about, maybe eighteen to nineteen lakh is the cash that actually reaches you over the year. The difference is not free money. It is money that locks up in your PF account, pays your employer risk, and buys you benefits. Understand this split before you accept any offer, because two offers with the same CTC can put very different cash in your hands.

### The Total Cost to Company Versus In Hand Pay

The CTC versus in hand pay gap matters most for two groups. First, if you live in a rented flat, your cash in hand determines how much you can invest every month, so a salary with a high PF share and high ESOP, which is employee stock option plan, content can feel poorer than its sticker price suggests. Second, if you are offered a big chunk of your package as ESOPs at a startup, the CTC looks wonderful, but that value is only real if the company exits or lists. We will get to ESOPs later in this chapter.

A practical rule: when you compare two offers, never compare the CTC numbers alone. Write down three numbers for each offer: the monthly cash in hand, the PF and gratuity accruals, and the equity value assuming a fifty percent haircut. Only then compare.

### Negotiating Your First Salary

Your first salary is the hardest negotiation of your life, because you have no market evidence of your worth. Before you negotiate, do three things. First, research your band. Indian platforms such as levels dot fyi, glassdoor, ambitionbox, and payslips shared in communities give you a range for your role, your city, and your years of experience. Second, pick a target number that sits at the top of that range, and use it as your opening number. Third, never say whatever you are comfortable with. Give a number, hold it for five seconds, and let the recruiter respond.

When the offer comes, ask for forty eight hours. Never accept or reject on the spot. Call the negotiation in one sentence: the offer is below my target band, can you review it? Do not leak your personal financial pressure. Do not say I need it for my rent. Say the market range for this role in Bengaluru or Pune is X to Y, and I would like the offer to land inside that range. If they cannot move the base, ask for a sign on bonus, a one time joining fee, or a faster confirmation date. Every piece of the offer is negotiable except your desperation.

### The Fifth Salary, When You Are Ready to Ask

The fourth salary you receive in your career is the one you negotiate when you have an outside offer in hand, or when you are ready to ask your current company to match one. The modern way to do this is called a counter. You apply quietly, you get a real offer of fifteen to twenty five percent more, and you show it to your current manager with a two week clock. Companies that care will respond. The honest question to ask yourself is whether your current company has ever had a salary revision policy that kept up with inflation and your growth. If the answer is no, your fifth salary is not a favor. It is a price correction. Do it every three to five years, and never on the basis of I have a pending loan.

### The Tenth Salary and Senior Level Moves

At senior and director level, the package shape changes. Your base is often forty to fifty percent of the total, your annual performance bonus can be thirty to fifty percent of your base, and ESOPs and executive equity can be the rest. In this range, the negotiation moves to a term sheet, not a chat. You negotiate a minimum base, a guaranteed bonus floor, a refresh of equity every year, and an accelerator clause if the company is acquired. The mistake most Indians make at this level is anchoring on last year's CTC, which is a lagging number. Anchor on the role's market rate, which is a leading number.

### Switching Jobs Versus Staying

Let's look at the real numbers. Inside an Indian organization, a yearly raise of five to ten percent is the norm for a steady performer, and a promotion can add ten to twenty percent. When you switch, you can historically expect a jump of fifteen to twenty five percent in a strong market, and ten to fifteen in a weak one. That means staying is never the cheapest path to a higher income. Over a ten year horizon, the difference between a switch every four years and a stay in one company is often two to three times what you ended with, once you count the promotions the switcher was priced into.

But there is a real cost to switching: you lose your internal context, your trust capital, and sometimes a better benefits package. The strategy is simple: stay long enough to build domain depth, usually three to five years, and switch when the depth becomes portable. A portable skill set is worth more than any single loyalty.

### The Lateral Move Ceiling

If you stay, you will eventually hit a wall: the company moves you laterally to a new team at a five to ten percent bump, and the wall of your grade band stops you. That is the lateral ceiling, and most careers quietly stall there for a decade. To break through, you stack skills that the band does not reward on its own: learn to be the engineer who understands product, the marketer who can read the P and L, the finance lead who speaks code. Portfolio of transferable skills plus one visible outcome that beat the band is what gets you promoted. The ceiling is not a salary number. It is the distance between what you do and what the next band demands.

### Negotiating a Tech or Product Role in 2026

The Indian tech hiring picture in 2026 is unusual. After two years of corrections, the roles that still command a premium are in AI infrastructure, large language model applications, applied machine learning, product management, and full stack engineering with a systems depth. Product managers with five years of experience in Bengaluru see bands of twenty five to forty five lakh, and top decile people in FAANG India, which means the big US tech firms, can see one to two crore plus a significant ESOP block. Indian startups at seed stage offer far less cash, often twelve to twenty two lakh, but the ESOP can be the difference maker if the company exits or lists within five years. If you are in data, security, or applied AI, you have leverage. Use it. If you are in a legacy web role, your 2026 negotiation is not a number. It is the gap between the skill of your last project and the skill of the next one.

### The Equity and ESOP Conversation

When a company offers ESOPs, walk through five questions with them. First, what is the strike price, which is the discounted price at which you can buy the share. Second, what is the vesting schedule, usually four years with a one year cliff, which means you get nothing in year one and then a tenth each quarter. Third, what happens to unvested options if you leave, and most plans forfeit them. Fourth, how does the ESOP sell at a liquidity event, and what are the tax numbers. Fifth, does the company have a buyback policy, and if not, an option that never sells is a line item on a pay slip, not an asset. In India, when you exercise options, the spread between the market value on the exercise day and your strike price is taxed as salary income. When you sell shares later, you pay capital gains, and if you hold for one year or more, the long term rate applies, which is far lower than your income tax slab. The ESOP conversation is not about what the company thinks it is worth. It is about what you can sell, when you can sell it, and what you owe the tax department on the way.

### Salary Sacrifice: When It Helps and When It Is a Trap

Salary sacrifice is the move where you give up part of your cash pay in exchange for a benefit, usually a tax efficient one. The common versions are: opting out of your own PF contribution to raise your in hand pay, and routing some cash into a personal NPS, which is the National Pension System, under the extra 80 CCD one B rule, which allows a fifty thousand rupee deduction on top of the normal limits even under the new tax regime. The PF opt out saves you about eight thousand to twelve thousand a month in cash flow, but reduces your retirement corpus, so it only works for people who are reinvesting the freed cash on their own, and even then you must check the PF interest against the equity you are buying. The NPS route genuinely works for high earners near the top slab, because a fifty thousand deduction at a thirty percent marginal rate saves you fifteen thousand in tax on money that earns a real market return. The trap version of salary sacrifice is when a startup asks you to give up base pay to buy ESOPs at a higher price, or when a bank sells you a structured investment that only earns if they sell a policy. Any sacrifice that does not show up as a real tax number or a real asset on your balance sheet is a trap. Sacrifice is only real when it moves money, not sentiment.

That is the full map of earning more in India: understand the band, anchor high, use market evidence, time your switch to break the ceiling, question the ESOP, and make sure every salary sacrifice is a math move and not a mood move.

## Chapter 7: Entrepreneurship and Side Income in India

The second engine of wealth is your second income stream, and in India, a side income is where most people who are not in the top five percent of earners start. The good news is that the legal structure for side income is simpler than most Indians think. The bad news is that the tax system quietly punishes the people who treat side income like an extension of their salary account, and that is where most of the problems come from.

### Sole Proprietorship Versus Partnership, LLP, and Private Limited

You have five legal ways to hold a side business in India, and the right one depends on how serious the income is and how much risk you are taking. The default, and the one most freelancers and consultants should start with, is a sole proprietorship. You do not register anything. Your side income is just your income, taxed at your personal slab, up to thirty percent plus cess. You can keep it in your personal account, but you should not, and we will explain why in a moment. A partnership firm adds a second partner and shares the profit and the liability between both, with no corporate registration needed, just a partnership deed. An LLP, which is a limited liability partnership, is where you buy a real shell: two partners, registered with the Ministry of Corporate Affairs, limited liability, and a separate legal entity, with a compliance cost of roughly twenty five thousand to fifty thousand a year in filings and professional fees. A one person company, or OPC, is the middle child: one director, one shareholder, a registered entity, minimum share capital of one lakh, and the same compliance cost as a private limited, plus a rule that you cannot have two directors. A private limited company is the full form: a separate legal person, investors can own you, and it is the only structure that serious venture capital funding lives in. The honest rule: if your side income is under ten or fifteen lakh a year and you are one person, stay a sole proprietor, get a current account, and be disciplined. If you are pulling in over twenty five lakh or you are taking a partner, register an LLP. If you are raising money from an angel or a fund, register a private limited from day one, not later.

### GST Registration: When You Must and When You Can Wait

GST, which is the goods and services tax, applies when your annual turnover crosses a threshold: twenty crore? No, twenty lakh for services and forty lakh for goods, with smaller limits of ten and five lakh in the special category states. Below the threshold, you do not have to register. But you can register even below it if your clients are GST registered businesses that need to claim input credit, or if you charge GST on the sales you make. A small side business that stays under the threshold and keeps receipts is not committing a crime. The trap is not skipping registration. The trap is skipping registration while invoicing GST registered clients who report their purchase to the tax department, and suddenly your sales are visible on their GST return but you are not registered. The moment your side income touches services to a registered business, register, or at minimum register under the composition scheme, which is a simplified GST regime with a very low tax rate, usually one to two percent depending on the year, in exchange for not charging GST.

### The One Person Company Option and Its Costs

If you like the OPC, read the cost line carefully. Registration is two to five thousand rupees in government fees plus professional charges of eight to fifteen thousand. Once registered, you file an annual return, a professional fee every year, and you have to do a basic audit if your income crosses certain limits, which adds twenty five thousand to sixty thousand a year. For a one person consultant earning forty to eighty lakh a year, a private limited or an OPC makes sense. For a tutor or a content creator earning fifteen to twenty lakh, the compliance cost eats a quarter of your profit, and a sole proprietorship with a separate bank account is cleaner. Do not register a shell entity for status. Register for the two real reasons: liability protection, and a clean line between personal and business money.

### Side Income Ideas That Actually Work in India

Let's be specific, because the Indian market rewards narrow, repeatable offers more than big, vague dreams. Tutoring is the most reliable starter income: mathematics, science, and JEE or NEET coaching can earn a good tutor anywhere from five hundred to two thousand a hour, and one part time tutor with ten to fifteen students a week is making fifty thousand a month. Freelance writing and web design for small businesses in Tier 2 and 3 cities is under supplied, and a portfolio of three or four real clients beats a polished LinkedIn. Content is a real but slow engine: a newsletter on a specific niche such as compliance, fintech operations, or a specific industry can build to fifty thousand to two lakh a month after eighteen months, not three months. Micro SaaS, which is a small software product with a monthly subscription, is the highest ceiling but the highest grind, and the realistic milestone is one thousand users by month twelve, not day one. Local services beat global ones for one reason: you can start today, and you do not need to beat a global price competition.
