Bookkeeping for small trades, estate management for a building, and wedding day coordination are all underserved by organized players in any Indian city. Pick one of these, build a repeatable offer, and price it for the Tier 1 or Tier 2 client you can close, not for the global client you have not met yet.

### Where to Keep Your Side Income So It Is Legal and Tax Compliant

The single biggest mistake is putting your side income in the same account as your salary, and then explaining it at tax filing time. The right setup is a second bank account, a current account if you are registered, or a separate savings account with its own card and its own UPI handle, which is the unified payments interface that most Indian apps use. All side income comes in there, all business expenses go out of it, and at the end of the financial year you pull one statement and give it to your CA, which is your chartered accountant. That is the entire workflow. Keep receipts in one folder, one per vendor. If you sell to a business that deducts TDS, which is tax deducted at source, you will receive a Form One Six A statement showing how much they withheld, and you need that for your return. The discipline here is not about the tax. It is about the habit of treating the side money as a separate life, so that when you scale, you are not rebuilding your records from memory.

### The Moonlighter Tax Trap

The specific trap that catches salaried Indians with side income is the mixed income problem. Your salary is already taxed by your employer at the TDS rate they estimated. Your side income is taxed on your own return. When the two land in the same income tax return, which is the document you file every year, your total income can jump you into the next slab, and the difference between slabs is real money. More importantly, if your employer's TDS estimate was low, which it usually is for people who have not told them about the second stream, you will owe the difference, and you will owe interest at one percent a month on the shortfall. The second trap is the audit trap: if your business side income is above a crore, or above a certain threshold for service income, and your profit is above three lakh, you can trigger a mandatory tax audit under the law, and that is a professional cost of fifty thousand to one lakh a year that most solo freelancers do not budget for. Before you scale, ask your CA the specific question: at my projected turnover and profit, am I above the audit line? If you are near the line, plan your pricing so you land just under it, because the audit is not a moral line, it is a cost line.

### When to Formalize Your Side Business

Use three trigger lines. The first line is twenty lakh a year in service income, which is the GST registration line for services, and it should trigger your first real conversation with a CA and your first business bank account if you do not have one. The second line is the point at which you are taking one serious client, which is the trigger to sign a written agreement and, if the income is above ten to fifteen lakh, to register an LLP or an OPC to get liability protection. The third line is the moment you hire a second person, because the moment you have a payroll, your personal account stops being the right home for this money, and you need a registered entity, statutory compliance, and a separation of the owner's personal money from the business's working money. Between these three lines, the gap is where most side businesses quietly die from sloppy records, because the person built income before they built the structure to hold it. Build the structure first, and the income gets to stay.

## Chapter 8: Understanding Indian Loans: The Good, the Bad, and the Ugly

A loan is a tool, and like every tool in India, it can build a home or break a family. This chapter is about knowing the difference, and about reading your loan offer the way a lawyer would, not the way a bank manager would.

### The Four Types of Loans Every Indian Should Know

There are four loans you will probably meet in your life. The home loan, the biggest and the most important, used to buy a flat or a plot. The auto loan, for a car or a two wheeler, which is the most common first installment. The personal loan, which is unsecured, meaning you do not put up collateral, and is used for weddings, medical emergencies, or debt consolidation, and it is the most expensive loan in the system. And the education loan, which is a government supported loan for a degree or a certification, with special tax rules we will cover in Chapter 9. Every loan has four numbers you must know before you sign: the interest rate, the tenure, the EMI, which is the equated monthly installment, and the total interest over the life of the loan. The total interest is the number the bank never prints on the front of the offer, and it is the number that decides whether the loan was a deal or a tax.

### How Interest Rates Actually Work: Fixed Versus Floating, MCLR, and the RBI Repo

All Indian loan rates, whether fixed or floating, trace back to one number: the RBI repo rate, which is the rate at which the Reserve Bank of India lends to banks. Banks set their own lending benchmark called the MCLR, which is the marginal cost of lending rate, and the MCLR is set at the repo rate plus a margin that reflects the bank's cost and its risk. When the RBI cuts the repo, the MCLR falls, and your floating home loan rate falls with it, usually by the same amount the next quarter. A fixed rate does not move with the MCLR, so if you fixed at eight and a half percent and the repo later fell to four and a half, you paid eight and a half the whole time. The rule: in a falling rate cycle, float. In a rising rate cycle, fix, or fix on a shorter tenure. The floating rate on your home loan is usually the MCLR plus a spread, and that spread is the number you can negotiate. A good spread is half a percent or less at the big banks, and a spread of one percent or more is a sign to shop.

### The EMI Trap: How to Read Your Loan Offer Letter

An offer letter has about forty lines, and you should read every one before you sign. The line that matters most is the interest rate and how it is described: is it MCLR plus a spread, or fixed, and is the spread in percent a year or percent a month. Banks sometimes write a flat rate, and a one percent flat a month is not the same as twelve percent a year on a reducing balance. If you see a one percent flat on a personal loan, the effective rate is around eleven to eleven and a half percent a year, and that is the number to compare with other offers. The second line to read is the prepayment penalty: many home loan banks charge you one to two percent if you prepay within five years, and some of the largest banks waive it entirely. The third line is the processing fee, usually a quarter to a percent of the loan, and it is negotiable, and the fourth line is the foreclosure penalty, which is what you pay if you close the loan before the tenure is over. And the fifth, and most overlooked line, is the mandatory insurance: the bank may require you to buy a term life insurance and a property insurance tied to the loan, and that is a real cost that shows up on your first year's statement, not on the offer sheet.

### Home Loans: SBI Versus Axis, ICICI, and HDFC

Let's be practical, because the four largest home loan lenders in India in 2026 are State Bank of India, Axis Bank, ICICI Bank, and HDFC Bank, and they are not the same bank with a different logo. SBI, which is the State Bank of India, usually has the lowest spread on its MCLR, no prepayment penalty on most of its home loan products, and the widest branch network, which matters if you live outside a Tier 1 city. Axis Bank prices aggressively on the retail side, especially for salaried professionals, and often waives the processing fee on a referral. ICICI is fast on the documentation and has a large top up loan culture, which we will cover later. HDFC, which is the Housing Development Finance Corporation, has the deepest book and the most consistent process, and its home loan usually has no prepayment penalty, which is the single most valuable feature on a long loan. The right approach is not to pick one bank and pray. It is to get the same loan amount from two or three banks, compare the processing fee, the prepayment charge, the top up eligibility, and the exact MCLR plus spread, and take the one that is cheapest over fifteen years, not the one that offered you the best cup of chai.

### Auto Loans: The Balance Transfer Game

Auto loan rates in India in 2026 are around seven to nine percent on a car, and lower on a two wheeler. The move that saves real money is the balance transfer: you took a loan at nine percent three years ago, and the market is now at seven and a half, and you transfer the remaining balance to the new bank at the lower rate. The math: on a five lakh loan with five years left, moving from nine percent to seven and a half saves you about twenty five thousand to thirty thousand over the remaining life, which is more than the one to two percent processing fee the new bank charges. The trap is the reverse: a new bank offers you a lower EMI by stretching your tenure from three years to five, and the total interest you pay goes up by more than you saved on the rate. Always compare total interest, not monthly EMI, when you do a balance transfer, and the number that matters is not the new rate, it is the total interest delta over the remaining balance.

### Personal Loans: The Most Expensive Loan and When to Avoid Them

A personal loan in India costs eleven to sixteen percent a year, with no collateral, and no tax benefit on the interest. That makes it the most expensive money in your house, more expensive than your home loan, your car loan, your education loan, and your overdraft. The only honest use of a personal loan is a short term bridge to a known date, which means you borrow for two to three months to cover a medical emergency and you pay it off from your next income. Every other use, a wedding, a vacation, a new phone, a down payment, is a loan you are taking at the worst rate to buy something that is not a return asset, and the math says no. If you are using one personal loan to pay another, stop, and call the first lender to ask about a restructuring or an extension, because the second loan will not reduce the first loan, it will increase it.

### Education Loans: The 2026 Rates and the Collateral Question

Education loans in 2026 are around eight and a half to ten percent a year, and they are the one loan where the government and the banks will talk to you kindly, because the borrower is a student and the guarantor is a parent. The collateral question is real: for loans up to about twenty five lakh, no collateral is needed, and for larger loans, most banks will ask for a gold lien, a fixed deposit lien, or an immovable property mortgage. The moratorium period is real: you do not pay EMI while you are studying, and for a few months after, but interest accrues during that time and gets capitalized at the end of the moratorium, so the loan balance is bigger when you start paying than it was when you borrowed. The tax benefit under section eighty E of the income tax law, which lets you deduct up to one lakh a year of education loan interest for eight years, is real, and it is the only loan in India where the interest is fully tax deductible for a long stretch of your working life, so if you are taking a higher degree loan, this is the one loan where the tax system quietly helps you.

### The Top Up Loan Feature on Home Loans

A top up loan is the feature where a home loan bank lets you draw additional money, up to a percentage of the principal you have already paid down, at the same home loan rate. So if you borrowed one crore and you have paid off two lakh, your bank will let you draw two to three lakh more at your home loan rate, which is eight percent or less, and use it for a kitchen, a renovation, or to consolidate a high cost personal loan. The trap is the marketing: the bank sells you the top up as a free feature, but it is still a loan, and the interest is not tax deductible for personal use, so a top up for a kitchen is taxed differently than a top up for a renovation of a rental property, and the tax person at your CA, which is your chartered accountant, is the one who decides which bucket you are in. Use the top up for a genuine consolidation of a high rate debt, and not for a stock position or a speculative bet, because you are borrowing at the home loan rate to buy something that can lose more than the interest you are paying.

## Chapter 9: When Borrowing Is the Right Decision

The previous chapter told you how to read a loan. This chapter tells you when borrowing is actually a good decision, because for most of your life, you will be borrowing, and the question is not whether you borrow, it is whether the loan is working for you or working against you.

### The Positive Spread Rule

The first rule is the positive spread rule: you only borrow when your investment return is likely to beat your loan interest rate, and the margin between them is bigger than the risk of the investment. If you borrow at nine percent to buy a rental property that yields three percent, the spread is negative, and you are losing money every year, and the only reason to do that is if the property appreciates faster than you lose, and even that is a bet, not a plan. If you borrow at eight percent to buy a business that earns you twelve percent on the invested capital, the spread is positive, and the loan is working. The rule is simple: match the risk of the asset to the cost of the debt. Borrow cheap for cheap, predictable, long term assets, and never borrow expensive for expensive, volatile, short term assets.

### The Home Loan as an Investment, the Real Math

The home loan is the most promoted loan in India, and the most misunderstood. The bank sells it as an asset purchase. The real math is this: you buy a one crore flat, you put twenty lakh down, and you borrow eighty lakh at eight and a half percent for thirty years, and your EMI is about seventy thousand a month. If the flat appreciates at three percent a year, which is a realistic long term number for an Indian metro, the loan balance falls much faster in the early years, because the EMI is mostly interest, and your equity in the property grows slowly. The spread is negative for the first five to seven years, and positive only after the loan is more than half repaid, or after the property appreciates faster than the interest cost. That is the real math, not the bank's brochure. The reason to take the loan is not the spread. It is the leverage: if the property appreciates at four to five percent a year over the full tenure, which some metros have done, your own twenty lakh down payment, which is what you actually put in, has grown to a much larger equity position than if you had just parked twenty lakh in a savings account. The loan is a leverage tool, and it is the right tool when you expect the asset to appreciate faster than the cost of debt, and it is the wrong tool when you are buying at the top of a cycle, which is the only time most Indians buy, because that is when the sales office is open.

### The Return on Investment of a Good Degree in 2026

An education loan is the one loan where the asset is you, and the return is not a rental or a dividend, it is a career premium. In 2026, the honest return on investment of a top MBA program, which is still about twenty four to twenty eight lakh in tuition at the best Indian business schools, against an average first year package of thirty to forty lakh at the same school, is a positive spread, but the spread is not as wide as it was ten years ago, because the private MBA sector is crowded and the average outcome is now closer to fifteen to twenty lakh a year, which is a much tighter spread against a ten to fifteen lakh loan. The rule for any degree: compare the loan cost against the premium salary over a non degree path, not against a non career path. If the premium over a non degree path is bigger than the loan interest cost over the loan period, the degree is a positive spread, and if the premium is smaller, the degree is a negative spread, and the loan is making you poorer every year until the salary catches up. A coding boot camp at four to six lakh, with a premium of fifteen to twenty lakh in a tech role, is a cleaner positive spread in 2026 than a mid tier MBA at twelve lakh with a premium of six to eight lakh, and the math is the same rule, just a different asset.

### Business Loans: When Debt Fuels Growth and When It Destroys

A business loan is the one loan where the spread is the point, because the asset is not a building, it is the business itself. The test is simple: what is the return on the specific asset you are buying, not the return on the business as a whole. If you are buying a machine that adds six lakh a year in revenue and the loan costs two lakh a year in interest, the spread is positive, and the loan is working. If you are taking a working capital loan at twelve percent to fund a new product line that has not made a first sale, the spread is unknown, and the loan is a bet dressed as a plan. The rule for business debt: never take a loan to fund a gap in your revenue, take a loan to fund an asset that closes the gap, and if the asset does not exist yet, the loan does not exist yet either. The second rule is the one that kills more small businesses in India than any other: never let your personal home loan and your business loan share the same repayment schedule, because when the business has a bad quarter, your house is the collateral, and the personal loan EMI is still due, and the two together can take more than your cash flow can cover.

### The Debt to Income Ratio and the 40 Percent Rule

The single number that tells you whether your loan stack is safe is your debt to income ratio, which is your total monthly EMI across all loans divided by your gross monthly income. The rule of thumb for Indian households is forty percent: if your total EMI is above forty percent of your gross income, you are in the danger zone, and any one income shock, a job loss, a medical event, a market correction, pushes you into default. The second test is the stress test: if your income halved for three months, could you still cover the EMI, your rent, your food, and your insurance premium? If the answer is no, you have over borrowed, and the fix is not to borrow more, it is to prepay the most expensive loan first, because the interest saved on the highest rate loan is the highest interest saving available to you, and it is the single fastest way to drop the debt to income ratio.

### Refinancing and Balance Transfer: When Two Percent Is Worth the Hassle

The last move is the refinance, and it is the one that saves real money for most Indian home loan holders in a falling rate cycle. The test: if your current rate is higher than the new rate by one and a half percent or more, and you have more than seven years of loan remaining, the savings over the remaining tenure are usually bigger than the one to two percent processing fee and prepayment charge you pay to move, and the transfer is worth it. The calculation is: take the remaining loan balance, multiply by the rate difference, and divide by two, and that is your rough annual saving, and if the annual saving is more than the one time cost, the transfer pays for itself in one to two years. The trap, which is the same trap as the auto loan, is the new bank stretching your tenure to lower the EMI, and you pay more total interest over a longer life. Compare total interest, not monthly EMI, on every refinance, and the number that matters is not the new rate, it is the total interest delta over the remaining balance, and if that delta is positive, do the transfer, and if it is negative, stay where you are and prepay instead.

## Chapter 10: The Dark Side of Indian Lending

We have talked about the good loans and the bad loans. Now we are going to talk about the ugly ones, and the ugly ones are not the ones you take, they are the ones that take you.

### How NBFCs and Online Lenders Prey on Desperate Borrowers

An NBFC, which is a non banking financial company, is a company that gives loans but is not a bank, and the difference is real: an NBFC does not hold deposits, which means it does not have the customer protection a bank has, and it does not have the same recovery rules a bank has, which means its collection behavior can be much more aggressive than a bank's. The online personal loan sector, which grew fastest in India between 2022 and 2025, is mostly NBFCs and a smaller set of banks with digital front ends, and the model is designed around one assumption: the borrower is desperate, and the borrower will not read the terms, and the borrower will not compare the offers. The rate they quote is the headline rate, the fee is the real cost, and the collection is the business. The fees stack: a processing fee of two to three percent at the door, a late fee on every missed EMI, a convenience fee on every payment you make through their app, and an annual maintenance fee that shows up as a fine print line in the offer letter. The effective cost of the loan, which is the rate that includes all the fees and the charges, is not the rate they advertised, and it can be twice or three times the advertised rate, and that is the number that should decide whether you signed.

### The Instant Loan App Trap

The specific trap is the instant loan app, the one that says you get a loan in three minutes, no documents, no branch visit. The three minute approval is real, and the three minute damage is real. The app checks your CIBIL score, which is your credit report from the credit bureau called CIBIL, and it also pulls your phone number list, which it uses for collection, and it asks for your device permissions, which it uses for data, and in the most aggressive cases, the app posts to your social media when you default, and it calls your contacts, which is the part of the experience that no term sheet will ever tell you about, because the term sheet is not the contract, the app's data and collection policy is the contract, and most people never read it. The cycle is: you default on one app, your CIBIL score drops, the next app offers you a worse deal, you take it to close the first, you default on the second, and the snowball rolls. The snowball only stops when you stop borrowing, and the moment to stop is before the third loan, not after, because after the third loan, the total debt is bigger than any single repayment, and the only exit is a restructuring conversation with every lender at once, and a police complaint if the collection has crossed the legal line.

### What to Do if You Are Already in an Unregulated Loan Trap

If you are in the trap today, the first move is the most important: no new loans to pay old loans, and if you have to borrow to stop the bleeding, borrow from a regulated bank, not from the same unregulated pool, because the regulated bank's collection is bound by RBI, which is the Reserve Bank of India, rules, and the unregulated pool's collection is not. The second move is the log book, which we will build next. The third move is the legal line: if a collection agent calls after eight in the evening, or on a Sunday, or to your employer or your family, or threatens you with language, or misrepresents their identity, that is a violation of the RBI's 2016 circular on loan recovery, which we will spell out, and you have a real legal remedy. The fourth move is the RBI Ombudsman, which is a free complaint channel for borrowers against banks and, to a limited extent, against regulated NBFCs, and the fifth move is the restructuring conversation, where you walk into your two biggest lenders and ask for a term extension, which lowers the EMI, and a reset of the repayment schedule, and the lenders will do it if you ask before you default, and they will not do it if you ask after you default, so the timing of the conversation matters more than the terms of it.

### The Log Book of Your Loans

The log book is the single most under used tool in Indian personal finance, and it is a single document, one page, one table, that you update every month. The columns are: the lender, the loan type, the original principal, the current balance, the interest rate, the EMI amount, the next EMI date, the prepayment penalty, the maturity date, and a notes line. The notes line is where you write the things that decide the future: a prepayment you made, a restructuring you agreed to, a call you had with the lender. The log book gives you two numbers that no app will give you: your total debt, which is the sum of the current balance column, and your total monthly EMI, which is the sum of the EMI column, and those two numbers are your debt to income ratio, and they are the only two numbers that matter when you are deciding whether to take a new loan, or prepay an old one, or restructure everything. The second benefit is the CIBIL benefit: if you find an error in your credit report, a late mark that is not yours, or a loan you do not remember, the log book is your evidence, and you can dispute it with CIBIL directly, and a clean CIBIL is worth more than any single loan decision you will ever make, because it is the number every future lender will look at first.

### The Legal Protections: RBI Guidelines on Loan Recovery and the 2016 Circular

The 2016 RBI circular on loan recovery is the document that sets the floor for how a recovery agent can treat you in India, and it is shorter than you would expect. The rules: no phone calls between eight in the evening and eight in the morning, no calls on a Sunday, no calls to anyone other than the borrower and the named guarantor, no physical visits to your home or your workplace, no misrepresentation of identity, no threat or intimidation, and no collection in the presence of a third party. The 2025 digital lending guidelines, which the RBI issued on top of the 2016 circular, extend the same floor to the app based lenders, and they add a cap on the effective interest rate that a digital personal loan can charge, and a requirement that the loan terms be disclosed before you accept, not after. The remedy if a lender crosses the floor: a written complaint to the lender first, then the RBI Ombudsman if the lender does not respond, then the consumer forum if the complaint is about a service deficiency, and a police complaint if the collection has crossed into intimidation or misrepresentation, which is a criminal matter, not a civil one. The key thing to understand is that the 2016 circular is not a ceiling on what a lender can do, it is a floor on what they must not do, and the difference between the two is the difference between a bad loan and a predatory loan, and the log book is the tool that tells you which one you are in.

### When to Talk to a Financial Advisor Versus a Loan Refinance Specialist

The last decision is who to call, and the answer is two different people for two different problems. If your problem is a single loan that is too expensive, the person you want is a loan refinance specialist, which is a person whose job is to move your loan to a cheaper lender, and the fee is a one time charge for the transfer, not a monthly fee, and the red flag is a specialist who asks for a monthly fee, because a transfer is a one time event, and a monthly fee on a one time event is a sign the person is selling your information, not the transfer. If your problem is the whole stack, which is a home loan, a car loan, two personal loans, a business loan, and a CIBIL score you do not understand, the person you want is a financial advisor, which is a CFA, which is a chartered financial analyst, or a CFP, which is a certified financial planner, who is paid a flat fee, not a commission, and who is not selling you a product, and the red flag is an advisor who will not tell you their fee in writing before the first meeting, because the fee structure is the first test of whether the person is working for you or for the product shelf.

## Chapter 11: Money and Marriage in India

This chapter is the one most Indian finance books avoid, and the one most Indian families need, because money in a marriage is not a separate conversation from the marriage, it is the marriage, and the way you handle it decides the shape of the next thirty years.

### The Dowry Culture and Its Financial Reality

Dowry, which is the transfer of money or property from the bride's family to the groom's family at marriage, is illegal in India under the dowry prohibition act of 1961, and the law is clear: the giving, taking, or demanding of dowry is a criminal offense, and the punishment is up to three years in prison. The reality is that the law has not stopped the practice, it has changed the form, and the form in 2026 is the wedding cost itself, which in a Tier 1 city can run from ten lakh to thirty lakh for a three day event, and in a Tier 2 city from five to twelve lakh, and the dowry is now the gold, the car, and the flat registration that the bride's family hands over, because the wedding is the event where the transfer happens, and the transfer is not a loan, it is a gift, and the gift is not taxed, which is the part of the system that makes it financially rational for the family that gives, and financially invisible to the tax department. The financial advice is not about the legality, it is about the budget: the wedding cost is a fixed number, and it should be a fixed number, and it should not be a loan, and if it is a loan, the loan is the most expensive debt in your life, because it is a debt that is not tax deductible, that does not produce an asset, and that is paid off over five to seven years while the couple is already carrying a home loan, which is the setup that breaks more young Indian marriages than any other financial event, and it is the one you can prevent by setting the budget in writing before the invite is printed, not after.

### Pre Nuptial Agreements in India: Are They Legally Binding

A pre nuptial agreement, which is a contract signed by two people before they marry that sets the rules for money in the marriage and the rules for money in a divorce, is not a recognized legal instrument in Indian family law the way it is in the United States, and the Indian courts have generally treated them as unenforceable, because the family law of India, whether Hindu, Muslim, or the Special Marriage Act, does not have a contractual pre nuptial regime. The recent shift is real but narrow: the Delhi High Court has, in a small number of cases, looked at a pre nuptial agreement and enforced the parts of it that are not about the divorce itself, which are the parts about alimony, property division, and spousal support, and the courts have said that an agreement on money between two adults is a contract, and a contract is enforceable, even if the agreement is not a divorce settlement. The practical advice is: do not sign a pre nuptial agreement and expect it to be your divorce plan, sign it as a money plan, and use it for what it can do, which is to set the rules for how the two of you will handle joint assets, how the two of you will handle separate assets, and how the two of you will handle a contribution from one family or the other, because those are the questions that the family law will not answer for you, and the agreement is the only place where you get to answer them in writing, before the emotion of the day decides the answer.

### Joint Accounts, Separate Accounts, and the Whose Money Is It Conversation

The question of whose money is it is the question that Indian couples do not ask, because the silence is assumed to be an answer, and the silence is not an answer, it is a debt. The structure that works in India is the two account structure: a joint account that is the family account, which is where the house, the kids, the insurance, and the shared investments live, and two separate accounts that are the personal accounts, which is where each partner's personal spending, their personal savings, and their personal guilt live, and the joint account is funded by a fixed percentage of each partner's income, which is the number that is set at the beginning of the marriage, not renegotiated every six months, because the renegotiation is where the conversation goes wrong. The second structure is the pot and the separate, which is the same as the two account structure but with a name: the pot is the joint account, and the separate is the separate account, and the rule is that the pot pays for the shared life, and the separate pays for the personal life, and the pot is funded by a percentage, and the separate is whatever is left. The third structure, which is the one most Indian couples actually use, is the one account, which is the husband's salary account, and it is not a structure, it is a default, and it is the default that creates the whose money is it conversation on the worst possible day, which is the day the relationship is already strained, and the way to prevent that day is to set the structure in the first year of the marriage, when the structure is easy, not in the fifth year, when the structure is the conversation.

### The Gold Purchase and the Wedding Cost Budget: The Real Numbers

The gold budget is the number that the Indian family knows better than any other number, and the number in 2026 is about one lakh to one and a half lakh per tola, which is ten and a half grams, because gold is at record territory in India, and a typical engagement and wedding purchase for a bride and a groom in a Tier 1 city is between two and three lakh total, which is the gold that is not the wedding, which is the gold that is the family's savings, and the wedding cost is the other number, and the two numbers together are the real cost of the marriage, and they are not the same number, and they should not be the same number, because the gold is an asset, and the wedding is an expense, and the family that treats the gold as an asset and the wedding as an expense has a different financial posture than the family that treats both as expenses, and the difference is the difference between a marriage that builds wealth and a marriage that spends it, and the question to ask before the purchase is not how much gold can we afford, it is what is the gold doing for us, and if the answer is status, the gold is an expense, and if the answer is wealth, the gold is an asset, and the two answers lead to two different purchase decisions, and the two different purchase decisions lead to two different family postures for the next thirty years.

### Post Wedding Money Setup: How to Split Finances Between Spouses

The setup that works in India is the monthly money meeting, which is a sixty minute conversation that happens on the first Sunday of every month, and the agenda is fixed: what did we spend, what is coming in the next month, what is the joint account balance, what is the separate account balance, and what is the one decision that needs both signatures, which is the decision above a fixed number, usually fifty thousand, which is the number that the two of you set in the first year, and the number that keeps the two of you from making a fifty lakh decision alone. The second element is the joint investment account, which is the SIP, which is the systematic investment plan, that the two of you fund from the joint account, and the SIP is not the only investment, it is the only investment that is both, which is the one that is the family's, and the third element is the term insurance, which is the life insurance that each partner carries, and the term insurance is not the same as the joint investment, it is the protection that the joint investment is built on, and the two of you should each carry a term insurance that is fifteen times your annual income, because that is the number that replaces your income for the family if you are not there, and the two policies together are the family's financial firewall, and the firewall is not optional, it is the structure that makes the rest of the structure possible.

### The In Law Money Question: Who Owns What When the Parents In Law Contribute

The question is the one that every Indian family that has taken help from its in laws has asked, and the answer is in the gift law, which is section 56 of the income tax act, which says that a gift from a relative is not taxable, and the relatives in the law include the spouse's parents, which means the in laws are relatives in the tax law, and the gift is not taxed, but the ownership of the gift is not decided by the tax law, the ownership of the gift is decided by who the gift is to, and the gift is to the person who the in laws named, and if the in laws named the son, the gift is the son's separate property, and if the in laws named both of them, the gift is the joint property, and the only way to know which one it is is the document that says which one it is, and the document is the gift deed, which is the one page that the in laws sign that says this money is for both of them, and the gift deed is not a legal formality, it is a family decision, and the family decision is the one that keeps the two of you on the same side of the table when the in laws' money is on the table.

### Divorce and Money: What Indian Law Actually Says About Asset Division

The last question is the one that no one plans for and everyone faces, and the Indian law on it is not what the movies say, because Indian law does not have a community property regime, which is the regime where the two partners' assets are split fifty fifty at divorce, and Indian law is the separate property regime, which is the regime where each partner keeps what is in their name, and the claims at divorce are the maintenance, which is the monthly support that one partner pays the other, and the child support, which is the monthly support for the child, and the property division, which is the division of the joint property, and the three claims are not the same claim, and the three claims are not the same amount, and the three claims are not decided by the same law, because the maintenance is decided by the personal law of the couple, and the child support is decided by the guardians and wards act or the personal law, and the property division is decided by the property law, and the three together are the divorce, and the three together are the only reason the two account structure, the gift deed, and the joint investment are not optional, because the three are the only documents that keep the two of you on the same side of the table when the table is the last one you are sitting at together.

## Chapter 12: The India Specific Money Map

This is the last chapter of Part Two, and it is the one that puts everything in one picture, because the instruments we have talked about, from the savings account to the home loan to the gold to the crypto, are not a list, they are a map, and the map is the only thing that tells you where you are, where you are going, and what you are not allowed to hold, and the map is the one you draw before you buy, not after.

### A One Page Mental Model of All the Money Instruments

The mental model has four rooms, and every instrument you own belongs in one room. The four rooms are: the safety room, which is the money you will never lose, which is the savings account, the fixed deposit, and the sovereign gold bond, and this is the room you park in, not the room you invest in. The second room is the growth room, which is the money you are trying to grow, which is the equity, the mutual funds, the index funds, the REITs, which are the real estate investment trusts, and the NPS, which is the National Pension System, and this is the room you grow in. The third room is the insurance room, which is the money that protects the other two rooms, which is the term insurance, the health insurance, and the personal accident policy, and this is the room you buy in, not the room you invest in. The fourth room is the alternative room, which is the money that is not in the other three, which is the crypto, the private credit, the direct real estate, the PMS, which is the portfolio management scheme, and the AIF, which is the alternative investment fund, and this is the room you sample from, not the room you fill. The four rooms together are the map, and the map is the one you draw before you buy, not after.

### Where Each Instrument Sits: Safety, Liquidity, Return, and Tax Efficiency

Every instrument has four coordinates, and the four are the only ones that matter. They are: safety, which is the probability you get your money back. Liquidity, which is the speed you can get your money back. Return, which is the number you grow with. And tax efficiency, which is the number you keep after the tax department takes its cut. You write these four on the one page, and the one page is the map, and the map is the one you look at before every purchase, not after.

To make it concrete: the safety of a sovereign gold bond is the government. The liquidity of a mutual fund is one to two business days. The return of an index fund is the market return minus a small fee. The tax efficiency of the NPS is a tax number, not a market number. The four together are the instrument, and the instrument is not the return, it is the return minus the tax. The return minus the tax is the number that grows your net worth, not the return that grows the statement. That difference is the difference between investing and gambling, and the map is the one that tells you which one you are doing.

### The Ladder from Savings Account to NPS to Direct Stocks to Crypto

The ladder is the order you climb, and the order is not the order of risk. It is the order of certainty. The certainty at each rung tells you how much you can put in before you move up.

The rungs are: one, the savings account, the bottom rung, the one you start on and do not park in. Two, the fixed deposit, the second rung, the one you move to when the savings account is full, because it pays you for the safety you already have. Three, the liquid fund, the third rung, the one that pays more than the fixed deposit and is just as safe, and it is the rung you climb when the fixed deposit is not enough. Four, the PPF, which is the public provident fund, and the NPS, the fourth rung, the one you climb for the tax benefit, not the return. Five, the mutual fund index, the fifth rung, the one that grows you, and the growth is the market, and the market is the one you do not time, you hold. Six, the direct stock, the sixth rung, the one you pick yourself, and the pick is the skill that separates the investor from the gambler. Seven, the REIT, the seventh rung, the one that adds a real estate return without a real estate loan. Eight, the sovereign gold bond, the rung that adds gold without the gold shop. Nine, the international fund, the rung that adds the currency hedge. And ten, the crypto, the rung you do not climb, you sample from, and the sample is one to five percent of your net worth, not more. The rungs together are the ladder, and the ladder is the map, and the map is the one you draw before you climb, not after.

### How the Instruments Interact

The instruments do not sit in one room each. They interact, and the interaction decides whether your portfolio is a portfolio or just a pile. There are four interactions to know.

The first is the equity and debt cushion. Equity and debt move in opposite directions: when the equity falls, the debt holds. That cushion is why you do not put everything in equity, and it is also why you do not put everything in debt. The balance between the two is the portfolio.

The second is cash drag. The savings account is not an investment, it is a holding, and a holding loses to inflation every year. The loss is why you park cash in the liquid fund or the fixed deposit instead of the savings account. Dead cash does not grow, alive cash does.

The third is the double count. This is the risk of holding the same asset in two instruments, such as the NPS and a mutual fund index that both hold the same three stocks. The double count is why you draw the map before you buy: the map tells you that two instruments that look different are really the same instrument, and the same instrument in two places is a pile, not a portfolio.

The fourth is the ESOP concentration. This is the risk of holding your company stock and your company ESOPs in the same portfolio. The two are the same exposure, so you do not hold them in equal amounts. When the ESOP vests, you sell it and move that money into the index fund, which is not the company. That is how a portfolio stays a portfolio.

### A Simple Money Allocation Personality Test

The test is five questions, and the five questions are the ones you answer honestly, and the honest answer is the one that is the map, and the map is the one you draw before you buy. Question one: if the market falls twenty percent in a year, what do you do, and the three answers are buy more, hold, and sell, and the buy more is the growth answer, the hold is the balanced answer, and the sell is the safety answer. Question two: what percent of your net worth is in cash and fixed deposits, and the three answers are more than forty percent, which is the safety answer, between fifteen and forty percent, which is the balanced answer, and less than fifteen percent, which is the growth answer. Question three: what percent of your net worth is in one company's stock, and the three answers are more than twenty percent, which is the concentration answer, between ten and twenty percent, which is the balanced answer, and less than ten percent, which is the growth answer, and the concentration answer is the one that changes your whole allocation, because the concentration is the risk that is not in the map yet. Question four: do you hold crypto, and the three answers are yes, more than five percent, which is the aggressive answer, yes, between one and five percent, which is the growth answer, and no, which is the safety answer. Question five: can you sleep if your portfolio is down thirty percent for a year, and the three answers are yes, which is the growth answer, maybe, which is the balanced answer, and no, which is the safety answer. The test is not a score, it is a map, and the map is the one you draw before you buy. The allocation it tells you is the one that is yours, not the one the market is selling you. The final question is the one that matters most, and it is not a number. It is: can you sleep when your portfolio is down thirty percent for a whole year? If the answer is yes, you are the growth type, and you stay the course. If it is maybe, you are the balanced type, and you hold the debt cushion. If it is no, you are the safety type, and you keep more in the fixed deposit and the sovereign gold bond. The down year is not the end. It is the next rung on the ladder, and you climb it because the map is the one that is yours, and the one that is yours is the one you keep.

That is Part Two. In Part Three, we will put the map to work: we will build the five year plan, the tax plan, and the retirement plan, and we will talk about the two instruments that are not on this map yet, which are the estate plan and the health plan. The health plan decides whether the estate plan is the one you pass on, and the estate plan decides whether the map you drew is the map your family draws, not the one the tax department draws for them.

# Part 3, Building Your Growth and Safety Engines

## Chapter 13: Mutual Funds, The Heart of Indian Investing

Let us start with the simplest possible picture, because mutual funds are the single most important tool in this whole book, and you should understand them the way you understand a two wheeler.

Imagine a group of twelve friends, each of whom has one lakh rupees to invest, but none of them is really sure which company to buy shares in. So they all pool their money. That is twelve lakh rupees. And they appoint one very smart, very experienced driver. The driver buys shares in a great many companies, a bit here, a bit there, in dozens of firms, big and small. Every friend can ride in the vehicle. No one has to drive alone, no one has to know everything about every company, and if one company breaks down, the whole vehicle does not stop. That is a mutual fund. The twelve friends are the investors. The pooled money is the fund. The smart driver is the fund manager. The vehicle is the portfolio. And every friend owns a tiny slice of the whole vehicle, not a slice of one broken wheel.

The word mutual in mutual fund means belonging to everyone. You are not buying a fund. You are buying a slice of it, the same slice that every other investor has. The fund is a basket, and when the companies inside the basket grow, your slice grows too. When they fall, it falls with them, gently, because the fall is spread across many companies instead of all landing on your shoulders.

Now let us meet the seven types of mutual funds that every Indian should know.

One, the large cap fund. Large cap means the biggest companies in India, the ones with the largest market value, the big blue chips that most people already know. Think of the very biggest names in banking, pharma, technology, and consumer goods. A large cap fund puts most of its money into the top one hundred companies by size. It is the slow, steady, big ship. It rarely sinks, but it rarely races ahead either.

Two, the flexi cap fund. Flexi means the manager has freedom. A flexi cap fund is not locked to big companies only. The manager can shift between large, mid, and small companies depending on where opportunities are best. If small companies look cheap, the manager goes small. If the big names look strong, the manager moves up. You are buying a decision maker who can move freely. In India, flexi cap funds have become very popular because they suit people who do not want to guess which size of company will do well.

Three, the mid cap fund. This is the middle tier. Companies that are smaller than the biggest names but bigger than the very smallest. They have more room to grow than the giants, but they are more established than the tiny newcomers. Mid cap funds can deliver stronger growth than large cap funds over a good stretch of years, but they also swing up and down more violently. They need a longer time horizon to work for you.

Four, the small cap fund. These are the smaller companies, the ones that can triple or quadruple in value if they execute well, but the ones that can also fall hard and stay hard for years. Small cap funds are high risk, high reward. You should treat them as a satellite, not your core. A small allocation, held for a long time, is the right way to use them.

Five, the debt fund. This is the quiet cousin. A debt fund lends money, mostly to the government or to safe companies, and earns interest on that lending. It is less risky and pays less than equity funds. Debt funds are where you park money you need in the next one to four years, or where a conservative investor keeps the stability layer of the portfolio.

Six, the hybrid fund. Hybrid means mixed. A hybrid fund holds some equity and some debt together, maybe sixty percent stocks and forty percent bonds, or some other mix. The classic example is the balanced advantage fund, which shifts the mix automatically based on how expensive or cheap the market looks. Hybrid funds are for people who want one product that handles both growth and stability.

Seven, the index fund. This is the special one, and it is the heart of Chapter Fourteen, so I will give it its full treatment there. In short, an index fund does not have a manager trying to pick winners. It simply copies a list, usually the biggest companies in India, in the same proportions. It is boring, it is cheap, and that is precisely why it tends to win.

Now the difference that saves you real money, the difference between the direct plan and the regular plan. This is one of the most important and most misunderstood things in Indian investing. When you buy a fund through a distributor, through a bank, through a financial advisor who is not paid by you, a commission is baked into the fund itself. You do not see it as a fee. It is hidden in the price. This is the regular plan. The distributor gets a one time commission from the fund house, and that cost stays inside the fund, lowering the returns that are actually credited to you, every single year, for as long as you hold it.

The direct plan is the same fund, run by the same manager, holding the same stocks, but bought without a middleman. No commission leaks out. You get the full return. The difference is small, maybe half a percent, maybe one percent a year, but it compounds for twenty years.

Let me show you the number. Suppose you invest one lakh rupees a year for twenty years, and the fund gives you a twelve percent return a year. If it is a regular plan that quietly leaks out half a percent a year, the effective return is one and a half points lower. Over two decades, the direct plan ends up worth around one crore ninety seven lakh rupees, while the regular plan ends up worth around one crore twenty eight lakh rupees. That is nearly seventy lakh rupees, about a small flat in a good part of Bengaluru, that simply walked out the door through the distributor s hidden commission. The same fund, the same manager, the same risks, and yet the direct plan gives you back the money the middleman was paid. This is why every financial planner in India who genuinely cares about your money will tell you the same thing. Buy direct plans.

Now, how do you read a fund fact sheet without getting lost? A fact sheet is a one or two page summary that the fund house must publish. There is no need to read every line. Look at four things. First, the expense ratio, which is the annual fee the fund charges you. Lower is better, especially in index and large cap funds, where the differences can be meaningful. Second, the average return, shown as the compound annual growth rate over three, five, and ten years. Third, the drawdown, which tells you how much the fund fell from its highest point in the past year. This is the honest number. Fourth, the asset allocation, which shows you what percentage is in stocks, what percentage is in debt, and what percentage is cash. If the fact sheet says it is an equity fund but shows a huge cash pile, that tells you something about the manager s conviction right now.

And now the star rating trap. Star ratings are given by the regulators and agencies, based on how a fund has performed in the past, relative to its peers. The trap is this. A fund that has been rated five stars has usually had a long stretch of strong past performance. That performance often came at a high price. The fund bought into a rally. It looked brilliant. And brilliant past performance is exactly the kind of thing that can reverse. Star ratings are a mirror, not a crystal ball. They tell you what already happened, not what will happen. A five star fund from a strong bull market may be a four star fund two years from now, or it may hold its ground. No one knows. So use stars only as a rough guide, and treat them with a healthy shrug. The single most important sentence in this entire book is this. Past performance does not predict future returns. Every other line you need to read I have already told you.

Next, systematic investment plan versus lump sum. A systematic investment plan, called SIP for short, is where you invest a fixed amount, say five thousand rupees, every month, instead of investing one lump amount all at once. It is the way most Indians start, and it is the right way for most people, for two reasons. The first is that it removes the stress of timing the market. You stop asking whether today is the right day to buy, because you buy every month whether the market is up or down. The second is something called rupee cost averaging. When the market is high, your monthly amount buys fewer units. When the market is low, it buys more. Over a few years, your average cost per unit tends to sit closer to the middle than to the top, which means you are not as likely to have bought everything at the very peak.

Lump sum, on the other hand, is when you have a chunk of money already, from a bonus, from selling property, from an inheritance, and you deploy it all at once. Research has shown that lump sum investing, because the market spends more of its time going up than down, has a statistical edge over a slow drip in. But the edge is modest, and it depends entirely on your nerves. If you cannot stomach watching your lump sum fall twenty percent in the first three months, you will panic and sell, and the edge disappears. For a regular person who earns a monthly salary, the SIP is the better fit, because that is how the money arrives, month by month.

How much should you invest through SIPs? A simple rule set. Start at five percent of your monthly take home income. If your monthly take home is fifty thousand rupees, that is twenty five hundred rupees a month, which is genuinely comfortable. Most people can manage it without changing their life. As your comfort grows, push toward ten percent. If you are young, single, or just starting out, twenty percent is a strong and sensible target. Twenty percent of a one lakh take home is twenty thousand a month, and that is where real compounding begins to bite. The percentage matters more than the absolute number, because it grows with you.

Finally, two options that often confuse people. The fund of funds is a mutual fund that does not buy stocks directly. It buys other mutual funds. It is useful when you want access to foreign markets through a single Indian product, or when you want a very simple single fund that does the mixing for you. The goal based fund is a fund designed to hit a target by a certain date, your child s marriage in eight years, a house down payment in five years. These are helpful tools, but they are not magic. They are just regular funds with a marketing frame around them, so they should be priced and judged exactly like any other fund, which is to say, cheap and boring, like an index fund.

## Chapter 14: Index Funds and the Case for Boring Investing

Here is the argument that will change how you think about investing. A very large share of the people who manage other people s money do not, over long stretches of time, beat the market that they are supposedly beating. The market here means the Nifty Fifty, which is the index of the top fifty companies in India by market value, or the Nifty Five Hundred, which is the top five hundred. This is not a claim that every single manager fails. It is a claim about the average, across many years, across many markets. Most active managers, on average, come up short of the index they are compared against.

In India, the Nifty Fifty has delivered, historically, around ten to twelve percent a year over long stretches, with big swings along the way. Some years it gave twenty five percent. Some years it gave negative fifteen. But averaged out, a decade, that is roughly the figure. And here is the quiet miracle. An index fund that simply tracks the Nifty Fifty, buying and holding the top fifty companies in proportion, has been able to capture that same average, without the cost of a manager trying to outsmart it, without the fee of a manager taking a cut, without the risk of a manager buying the wrong things at the wrong time.

The numbers say this. If you take a basket of active large cap and flexi cap funds in India and compare them to the Nifty Fifty index over a ten year window, a large majority of the funds will come in below the index, when you include their fees. Some funds beat it, but not enough, over long enough, to make it likely that a random pick will. You could close your eyes, point at a fund, and do better by pointing at the index instead. That is the case for boring investing. Boring is beautiful, because the index is cheap, it is tax efficient in the same way every other equity fund is, it is transparent, and it has no manager risk. The only risk you take is the risk of the whole market, which is the risk you were going to take no matter what, because you cannot avoid the overall market by buying individual active funds.

How to start with an index fund SIP in under thirty minutes. First, open an account with a discount broker, or open one directly with a fund house s platform. If you have a discount broker, you can access index funds through them at a low cost. Second, pick a Nifty Fifty index fund with a direct plan, which we covered in the last chapter. Third, set up a monthly SIP of whatever amount you have decided on, say two thousand or five thousand rupees. Fourth, link your bank account for auto debit. Fifth, confirm. You have now invested in the top fifty companies of India, at a cost that is a fraction of an active fund, and you will never need to watch the ticker again.

The core and satellite strategy is a natural extension. Put seventy percent of your equity money into boring index funds. That is your core, the foundation, the thing you will never touch. Put the other thirty percent into the active funds you actually believe in, the mid cap funds with a manager you trust, the small cap funds you want exposure to. The satellite is where you take some risk, some taste, some belief. The core is where you let the boring do its work. If you have no strong beliefs about which active manager will win, push the core to eighty or ninety percent, and let the satellite be very small, or zero.

Now the tax treatment, because people panic about this. Long term capital gains, called LTCG, on equity funds, including index funds, are taxed at a flat rate above a threshold, and the threshold is one lakh rupees a year. That means if your gains across the year are under one lakh, you pay nothing. If they are above, you pay the flat rate on the amount above. Short term capital gains, called STCG, which is what applies when you have held the fund for less than a year, are added to your income and taxed at your slab rate. Dividends from index funds, if you take them, are taxed in the investor s hands, not the fund s, under the current regime, so you are paying tax on the dividend amount at your own slab. The key insight is that index funds, because they do not trade as much as active funds, have very little short term capital gains being generated inside them, so most of your gains, when you finally sell, will be long term, which is the cheaper bucket. That is one of the quiet, structural advantages of boring.

The final and hardest part is the discipline. When the market drops twenty percent in a quarter, your index fund is down twenty percent. Your gut says sell, wait for a bottom, buy back cheaper. But you cannot time the bottom, no one can, and selling a little before the bottom and buying back after it has already recovered is exactly how people lock in a loss and then regret it for a decade. The rule you build is simple and unsexy. You do not sell the core because it went down. You check your allocation. If equity has dropped below the percentage you want, you actually add more, not less, because the index is now offering you its best entry price of the year. You review once a quarter, not daily. You close the app. You let time do the work, and you let boring be beautiful.

## Chapter 15: Direct Stock Investing, The Right Way for Indians

There is a difference between trading and investing that most Indians, especially the ones glued to their phone at nine in the morning when the market opens, have not quite internalized. Trading means you are trying to buy low and sell high inside a window that might be days, or weeks, or a few months. You are trying to pick the exact dips and the exact peaks. Investing means you buy a good business at a fair price, you hold it for years, and you let the business grow. These are two different jobs, with two different skill sets, and two very different success rates. And the success rate for retail traders in India, the regular people with phone apps, is unflattering. The majority of them, when you count them over a full market cycle, lose money, because they are paying brokerage, they are paying taxes on every round trip, they are chasing momentum, and they are the last ones out of the boat when the music stops. The people who actually make money in Indian markets, over the long run, are not the day traders. They are the people who found a good company, bought it at a reasonable price, and stayed for years through at least one bad stretch.

How to open a demat account and what it really costs. A demat account, short for a dematerialised account, is where your shares live, in electronic form, with a depository. To open one, you need a Permanent Account Number, called PAN, which is the tax identifier every Indian is given, and a bank account. You open it through a broker. There are two kinds. A full service broker, which is a bank s stock broking arm or a full service house, and a discount broker, which is the app based, low cost kind that most young Indian investors use now. The cost picture looks like this. For a discount broker, the cost of a small trade, say five thousand rupees, is maybe fifty rupees per executed order, plus a few rupees of regulatory and stamp duty charges, plus the exchange transaction fee, which is a tiny fraction of the trade value, plus the Goods and Services Tax, called GST, on the brokerage. For a full service broker, the cost can be one or two percent of the trade value, which is a meaningful tax on your returns if you trade often. The bigger point is that every time you buy and sell, you pay, and those payments, added up across hundreds of round trips, are why so many active traders bleed money even when their trades are technically correct.

Now, fundamental analysis in fifteen minutes. When a stock is pushed your way, you do not need to read a hundred page annual report. You need to run a quick five point check. One, look at the revenue. Is the company s sales going up, and are they going up faster than the inflation of the economy? A company whose sales are flat or shrinking is a company that is losing ground, no matter how cheap the stock looks. Two, look at the debt. How much does the company owe, and how fast does it make cash to pay it back? A healthy company can repay its debt quickly. A shaky one cannot. If the debt is huge and the cash is small, walk away. Three, look at the return on capital employed, called ROCE. This is the percentage the company earns on the money it has put to work. A good company earns a high, steady percentage on its capital, and a very good one does it year after year. ROCE is one of the cleanest numbers you will ever look at, because it tells you how efficiently the business converts money into more money. Four, look at the promoter holding, which is the percentage of the company owned by the people who started it or are in charge. When the people with the most skin in the game hold a lot of the company, that is reassuring. When the insiders are selling, that is a whisper you should hear. Five, look at customer concentration. How much of the company s business comes from one big customer? If one customer is forty percent of sales and that customer walks, the company collapses. A diversified customer base is a moat.

The three question filter, before you buy any stock. One, can I explain in two sentences why this business is valuable, and why the value will keep growing for a long time? Two, is the current price a reasonable or cheap entry, or am I paying a premium for a story that has already run ahead of the numbers? Three, if this stock does not move at all for two years, would I still be happy to hold it, because the business underneath is still getting better? If you can answer all three of these clearly, you are in investor territory. If you cannot, you are in trader territory, and you should know that.

Position sizing, why putting forty percent in one stock is dangerous. Even a brilliant company can have a bad two years. If you have put forty percent of your investable money into one stock, and it halves, you have lost twenty percent of everything, and you now need a two hundred percent gain just to get back to where you started. Most companies, in a normal cycle, do not give you two hundred percent in the time you have to wait. The rule is simple. No single stock should be more than ten to fifteen percent of your equity portfolio, except in rare, deep conviction cases, and even then, cap it. If you truly believe in one company, you can put twenty percent, not forty. Diversification is not for people who think they are smarter than the market. It is for people who know that even good businesses have bad years.

The portfolio review habit. Do it quarterly, not daily. Open your portfolio, look at your positions, check that each one still passes the three question filter, rebalance if one position has grown to an uncomfortably large share, and close the app. Do not open the app to check the price at nine in the morning. The app is for rebalancing, not for anxiety.

Finally, the stock picking versus fund picking decision tree. Ask yourself one question. Do I have the time, the temperament, and the skill to read five or ten businesses properly and hold them through a bad year? If the answer is yes, you can put a portion of your equity money into individual stocks, and the rest in funds. If the answer is no, and for most people the answer is no, you should be in index funds and a small number of active funds you have studied carefully, and you should leave the individual stock buying for very small, exploratory positions, or not at all. There is no shame in the second path. It is the path that a large share of the successful long term Indian investors actually walk.

## Chapter 16: Bonds, Debentures, and the Fixed Income Ladder

Fixed income is the part of the portfolio that whispers instead of shouts, and it is the part that keeps you asleep when the equity market has a bad quarter. In India, the universe of fixed income, if you are not a professional, mostly comes in a few flavours.

First, the government securities, called G Secs. These are bonds issued by the central government, which means the borrower is the Government of India. If you lend to the government, the risk of the government not paying you back is, for practical purposes, near zero, inside India s currency. The only risk is that the value of what you are paid loses purchasing power if inflation runs hot, but the risk of default is minimal. These are the safest bonds you can own in the Indian market, short of cash itself.

Second, the state development loans, called SDLs. These are bonds issued by the state governments, the governments of individual states. They are a notch riskier than the central government s bonds, because a state s finances are smaller and can be weaker than the central government s, but they are still very safe, and they tend to pay a little more than the central government s bonds, to compensate for that extra sliver of risk.

Third, the public sector banks, called PSBs, which are the government owned banks, and the bonds they issue. These are a bit riskier still, because the banks, even the government ones, are businesses that can be stressed, but the government usually stands behind them. They pay more, and they are a sensible rung on the fixed income ladder for the middle to higher risk section of your savings.

Fourth, the corporate bonds. These are bonds issued by companies. The company promises to pay you back with interest, at a set date, and in return, you get a higher yield than the government gives you, because you are taking on the risk that the company might not be able to pay. The higher the credit rating of the company, the lower the risk and the lower the yield. The lower the rating, the higher the yield and the higher the risk. This is where the trade off gets sharp, and it is where most of the real decisions in fixed income are made.

How to buy government bonds directly, without a fund house in the middle. The Reserve Bank of India, called the RBI, runs a portal called Retail Direct, where any Indian with a bank account can buy government securities, and the state development loans, directly, without paying a broker. You open an account on the portal, you choose a bond, you pick a maturity, you pay, and the bond sits in your account, paying you interest, or paying you back at maturity, depending on the type. It is genuinely one of the cleanest, most self serve things the Indian government has built for regular people, and it is free or nearly free to use.

The laddering strategy, which is the core fixed income move. Instead of buying one big bond that matures in ten years, you buy several smaller bonds that mature in one, three, five, and seven years, staggered. So every year, or every couple of years, a chunk of your ladder matures, and you can either collect that money or reinvest it at whatever rate is current. This has two benefits. One, you always have some money coming back, so you are not locked in for a decade. Two, you can reinvest at the current rate, so if rates rise, you capture the higher new rate on the portion that has matured, and you are not stuck with an old, lower rate for the full ten years. The ladder smooths out the interest rate risk, and it gives you flexibility. It is the single most useful structure you can build with bonds, and it is not hard to build.

The punch card of what yields you should expect, as of writing. Think of these as rough guideposts, not promises. Government securities, for a medium to long tenor, are paying somewhere in the seven to seven point five percent range, moving with the central bank s policy decisions. State development loans, a half point or so above that. Public sector bank bonds, maybe another point or two above, depending on the specific issue. Corporate bonds from very large, safe companies, in the high seven to low eight percent range, and corporate bonds from mid rated companies, in the mid to high eight percent range, with the riskier, lower rated ones pushing into the low to mid nine percent range. The punch card tells you what is normal, so that when someone offers you eleven percent on a bond that claims to be a safe AAA, you know to run, because that is not what AAA is supposed to pay, and whatever is paying you eleven percent is doing something you do not want to be on the receiving end of.

The decision between a debt fund, a fixed deposit, and a bond. A fixed deposit, or FD, is a deposit at your bank, a known rate, a known date, insured up to a limit by the deposit insurance. It is the simplest option, and for a small amount, it is fine. The catch is that the interest is taxed in your hands, every year, at your income tax slab, so your effective return after tax is lower than the headline rate. A debt fund is a fund of bonds. It is more flexible, you can redeem any time, but the pricing moves with interest rates, so a debt fund held for a short time can lose money, and the gains you make are still taxed, in a way that is more complex than an FD. A direct bond is the cleanest tax and structure tool, you hold it to maturity, you get your coupon, you get your principal, and you can use a ladder to manage reinvestment. For a conservative saver with a few lakh rupees, an FD is fine. For a saver with a few crore or more, a direct bond ladder on Retail Direct is better, because the tax treatment of holding to maturity is cleaner, and the rates are often a point or two above what the same bank will give you on an FD. For the middle, a debt fund, or a mix, is a reasonable middle ground.

The inflation linked bonds, called ILBs. These are government bonds whose payments are adjusted by the consumer price index, so that the amount you get back grows with inflation. In a year where inflation is high, the ILB is doing its job, protecting your real value. In a year where inflation is low and nominal rates are high, a regular bond can pay more than the ILB. The ILB wins when you genuinely expect a stretch of high inflation, and it is the natural tool for the very conservative, very long term corner of the portfolio, the money you will not touch for fifteen years and that you want to protect from the slow erosion of the rupee s value.

## Chapter 17: PPF, EPF, NPS, and the Government Pension Stack

This chapter is about the three pillars of the government s retirement scheme, and how to use them together without getting the rules wrong.

The Public Provident Fund, called PPF. The rules are simple. You can put in between one thousand and one lakh two rupees a year, and you lock it in for a period of fifteen years, after which it matures. You can do partial withdrawals after a certain number of years, subject to the rules, which are the partial withdrawal trick that a lot of Indians use to fund a specific goal without breaking the account. The tax benefit is three layered. The amount you contribute is deductible under Section Eighty C, up to the overall limit of one lakh five rupees a year, which is shared with every other Eighty C investment. The interest it earns is exempt from tax, every year, while it sits in the account. And the final amount you get at maturity is also tax free. That is the triple tax shield, and it is what makes PPF one of the best risk adjusted returns in the whole Indian system, especially if you believe the interest rate will hold near where it is. As of writing, the PPF interest rate is set by the government, and it has been in the low to mid seven percent range in recent quarters, revised every quarter.

The Employees Provident Fund, called EPF. This is the automatic retirement savings for anyone on a salary, who is on a formal pay roll with an employer. The employer and the employee each contribute a set percentage of the employee s basic pay, into the employee s EPF account. The employer s contribution is also a deduction for the employee, under the tax rules, which is a benefit most people do not fully appreciate. The EPF account matures when you leave your job, or when you retire. There is a five year rule, which means that to withdraw the full amount tax free, you generally need to have been a contributing member for at least five years. Before that, you have limited withdrawal options, for specific things like marriage, home purchase, medical treatment, or education of children. There are also partial withdrawal options for specific purposes, and the PF loan option, which is a loan you can take out of your own EPF balance, against specific purposes, with the interest coming out of your own balance. The temptation is real, and it is worth naming. A PF loan feels easy, because it is your own money, and the interest rate is lower than a bank loan, so people take it out for a car, or for a family trip, or for a wedding. But the interest is not free. It comes out of your own retirement corpus, and every year you take a PF loan is a year that the compounding does not run on that money. If you can avoid the PF loan, avoid it. It is the one shortcut in the system that quietly costs you the most over a career.

The National Pension System, called NPS. This is the voluntary, second pillar of the pension stack.
