You open an account, you contribute, and the money is invested, mostly in the way you choose, between equity, corporate debt, and government debt, based on your age and your risk tolerance. There is a 60 40 0 rule, which is the simple version of how the allocation should shift as you get older. In your twenties and thirties, sixty percent of your NPS money can be in equity, because you have a long time to ride out a bad year. In your forties and fifties, shift toward forty percent equity, sixty percent debt. In your final years before retirement, go to zero equity, all debt, because you cannot afford a bad equity year in the last stretch before you start taking the money out. That is the 60 40 0, and it is a rule of thumb, not a law, but it is a good one to follow.

The employer contribution, which is the part most people miss. If you have an employer who contributes to your NPS, that contribution is an extra layer on top of your own, and it is a genuine benefit, because it is money you are not paying out of your own pocket, that is going into your retirement. If your employer does not contribute, that is still fine, you just do not get the free layer.

The eighty CCD, 1B benefit. This is the one that makes NPS attractive, even if you are not a fan of the whole system. You can claim a deduction of up to fifty thousand rupees a year, on top of the one lakh five thousand Eighty C limit, specifically for NPS contributions, under Section Eighty CCD One B. That is an extra fifty thousand rupees of tax saving, that you can get even if you are not using your full Eighty C limit elsewhere, which is a very real reason to have an NPS account, even if it is a small one.

The tax free withdrawal question. This is the most controversial rule in the whole system. When you exit NPS, at the age of sixty, or earlier in specific circumstances, you must take out a certain percentage of the final corpus as a lump sum, and that lump sum is taxed, unless you have elected a tax free exit under certain conditions, which are specific and not universal. The remaining percentage is used to buy an annuity, which is a product that pays you a fixed amount every month for life, and the annuity payments are tax free. The exact percentages and the exact tax treatment have been the subject of amendments, so the cleanest advice is to verify the current rule with a tax advisor in the year you actually exit, because the rule in the year you exit is the rule that matters, and it has changed more than once.

The triple stack, how PPF, EPF, and NPS work together. PPF is the long, safe, tax free core. EPF is the automatic, job linked, tax efficient layer you get for being on a pay roll. NPS is the flexible, tax incentive layer that lets you take the extra fifty thousand deduction. The three of them, run in parallel, for a salaried person, create a retirement corpus that is diversified, that is tax efficient, and that is not dependent on any single asset class or any single employer. When you add your equity mutual funds and your direct bonds on top, you have a full, working personal finance system, and it is mostly built on the back of the government s own pension architecture.

The NPS exit calculation, what you actually get at sixty. You do not get the whole corpus in one free lump sum. You get a portion, say fifty percent, as a lump sum, which may or may not be taxed, depending on the rules in effect and your election, and you must use the other portion to buy an annuity, which then pays you a fixed monthly amount for life. The monthly amount depends on the annuity rate, which fluctuates with interest rates, and on the life expectancy table the insurer uses. If you have a corpus of one crore rupees at sixty, and you use half of it, that is fifty lakh rupees, to buy an annuity, at a typical annuity rate in the mid to high six percent range, that is roughly a monthly pension in the range of twenty to twenty five thousand rupees, for life. That is not a lot, which is why the NPS is a supplement, not a replacement, for your own investment corpus, and why the equity side of your portfolio, the mutual funds and the index funds, has to do the heavy lifting. The NPS is the floor. Your investments are the ceiling.

## Chapter 18: Real Estate in India, The Dream and the Debt

Real estate is the one asset class that every Indian family treats as the default answer to every financial question. Buy land in your village. Buy a flat in the city. Buy a shop for the rental income. And yet, for most Indian households, real estate has been, by the numbers, a poorer performer than the stock market, and a much less liquid one, and a much more debt dependent one. Let us separate the dream from the debt.

The rent versus buy math, for the six major cities, as of writing. These are rough, approximate numbers, because the market moves fast and the exact figure depends on the specific street, the builder, and the month. In Delhi NCR, specifically the outer sectors of Gurgaon or Noida, a two bedroom apartment costs somewhere in the range of sixty five to ninety lakh rupees, and the rent for the same apartment is in the range of thirty five thousand to fifty thousand rupees a month. In Mumbai, the outer suburbs, a two bedroom flat is in the range of ninety lakh to one and a half crore, and the rent is twenty five thousand to forty thousand a month. In Bengaluru, in the newer corridors, a two bedroom flat is in the range of one crore to one and a half crore, and the rent is thirty to fifty thousand a month. In Hyderabad, in the growing corridors, a two bedroom flat is in the range of fifty to eighty lakh, and the rent is twenty to thirty five thousand a month. In Pune, in the newer areas, a two bedroom flat is in the range of seventy to one crore, and the rent is twenty five to forty thousand a month. In Chennai, in the developing corridors, a two bedroom flat is in the range of sixty to ninety lakh, and the rent is twenty to thirty five thousand a month.

Now do the simple math. If you rent, you pay the rent out of your pocket every month. If you buy, you take a loan, and the loan is, for a sixty lakh flat at a home loan rate in the mid to high eight percent range, roughly forty lakh borrowed, which means a monthly payment in the range of twenty five to thirty five thousand, over twenty years. The difference between the monthly rent and the monthly loan payment is the real cost of buying versus renting, for the month. But that is only one part of the equation. The other part is what you do with the difference. If you rent, and you invest the difference in the stock market or in index funds, at a reasonable return, and the property you would have bought only appreciated at, say, eight to ten percent a year, the renting side, plus the invested difference, can actually beat the buying side, in wealth terms, after you have factored in the loan, the property tax, the maintenance, and the fact that you cannot sell a flat quickly without a haircut. This is not a universal truth. In some cities, in some corridors, the appreciation has been strong enough that buying wins. But the calculation is worth doing, before you assume buying is the default.

The first property checklist, what to check before you sign. One, is the builder registered with RERA, the Real Estate Regulatory Authority, which is the regulator that was set up to protect buyers of new and under construction property, and if the builder is not registered, walk away, no matter how good the brochure is. Two, is the specific project registered under RERA, with the approved plans, and is the registration number on the builder s website, or is it only in a WhatsApp forward from a relative. Three, is the land title clean, is it free of litigation, is it not held by the court or by another owner s claim. Four, is the structure approved by the local municipal authority, and is the construction in line with that approval. Five, what is the carpet area versus the built up area, because the builder quotes you on one and hands you the other, and the difference is where a lot of the hidden cost hides. Six, what are the maintenance charges, and who pays for the common areas, and for how long. Seven, is the builder financially sound, are they running several projects at once, and can you verify, through a simple search, that they have not been the subject of a delay complaint or a court case.

The under construction risk, and the RERA protection. Buying under construction is where most of the pain happens, because you are paying for a property that does not exist yet, and you are trusting the builder to finish it. RERA was created, specifically, to reduce that risk. It requires the builder to hold the buyer s money, for that specific project, in a separate account, so that if the project stalls, the money is not free to run to another project. It requires the builder to disclose delays, to give possession by a set date, and to compensate the buyer if the delay passes a threshold. RERA is real, and it is useful, but it is not perfect. The protection depends on the builder actually being registered, and on the account actually being kept separate, and on you actually filing a complaint if the delay happens. If you are buying under construction, assume you are taking a real risk, and price that risk into your decision, and keep the RERA number close, because you may need it.

The home loan as an asset class. Yes, it is an asset class, and it is a strange one, because it is a loan, and a loan is usually a liability. But the home loan, in India, gets a tax benefit on the interest paid, up to a limit under Section Twenty four B, which means that a portion of the interest you pay is deductible from your taxable income. That is a real, annual, tax saving, and it is one of the reasons the home loan has been a popular instrument for decades. The catch is that the benefit is on the interest, not on the principal, and the benefit is only useful if you are in a tax slab high enough to actually use the deduction. If you are in the lowest slabs, the benefit is small, and the math is not as compelling. And the home loan is not a free loan. It is a twenty year, eight to nine percent liability, and it should be used to buy an asset that you will genuinely live in for a long stretch, not to buy an investment property you cannot afford.

The rental yield in India, and why it is so low. A rental yield is the rent you can get on a property, divided by the price of the property, times one hundred. In most Indian cities, the yield on a residential flat is in the range of two to three percent a year. That is low, by global standards, where yields are often in the four to six percent range, and it is low enough that the rental income, on a residential flat, is not a serious income stream, it is a small, annoying, maintenance dependent trickle. The reason it is low is that the capital appreciation in Indian property has historically been so strong, that the price of the flat is so high, relative to the rent, that the rent is a small percentage of the price. You are paying for the capital gain, not for the income, and that is why, if your goal is income, a residential flat is the wrong instrument, and a commercial property is the right one.

The vacant land trap. This is the one that loses the most Indians the most money, quietly. You buy a plot of land, in a town or a village, because the price is low, and because a relative said the area is going up. And then you wait. And the area does not go up, or it goes up very slowly, or it goes up and then goes down, because the specific plot was not the one that was supposed to be developed, or because the road that was supposed to come never came, or because the title had a quiet defect that only shows up when you try to sell. Land is the most illiquid asset in the system. It takes months, sometimes years, to sell. It takes real money to maintain, in the form of property tax and boundary care. It has no rental income, so it is a pure carry cost, every year. And it is the one asset where the legal risk is highest, because the title can be disputed, because the encroachment can happen, because the boundary can be challenged. If you buy land, buy it in a developing corridor with a clear, verifiable title, and buy it with a realistic expectation that it will take a decade, not three years, to be worth the effort of selling.

The commercial property option. When a small office, or a shop, is better than a house. A small commercial property, a shop in a growing area, or a small office space, is different from a residential flat, in two ways that matter. One, the rental yield on commercial property is often in the three to five percent range, which is meaningfully higher than residential, because commercial tenants pay more, and the lease is longer and more stable. Two, the capital appreciation on commercial property is often driven by the local economy, by the number of businesses and foot traffic in the specific area, not by the general appreciation of the city. If you are looking for income, commercial property, in a genuine growth corridor, is a real option, and it is worth the extra effort of finding it, and of verifying the footfall, and of checking the lease terms. But it is a business, not a savings, and it should be run as one, with a real underwriting, not as a default.

## Chapter 19: Gold, Fixed Deposits, and the Safe Corner

Gold is the safe corner of the Indian portfolio, and it is not just a superstition. It is a very old, very deep, very rational part of the Indian financial instinct, and it deserves to be in the portfolio, in the right amount, in the right form.

The Indian gold culture, and the real math. Indians have worn, bought, and buried gold for millennia, and the culture is not going away. The question is not whether to own gold, it is how much, and in what form, and at what cost. The real math of gold, in India, is complicated by two things. One, the making charge, which is the cost of the craftsmanship, and which can add fifteen to thirty percent on top of the raw gold price, when you buy a physical piece of jewellery. Two, the import duty and the GST on gold, which add a layer of tax to every gram that crosses the border, and which means that the price of gold in India is structurally higher than the world price of gold, by the margin of those taxes. So when you buy physical gold jewellery, you are paying the world price, plus the import tax, plus the making charge, and when you sell it back, you get the world price, minus the making charge, and you get back the making charge, and the taxes, so the round trip cost of physical jewellery is high, and it is a real drag on the return.

The four options, compared. One, physical gold, in coins or bars, which is the cheapest physical form, because there is no making charge, and the spread between the buy and sell price is small, and it is the form that is easiest to store safely, if you have a safe. Two, digital gold, which is gold you buy through an app or a bank s platform, in small quantities, and which is stored, on your behalf, in a vault, and which you can sell back at any time, at a small spread. Three, the gold exchange traded fund, called a gold ETF, which is a fund that tracks the price of gold, which you can buy and sell in the stock market like any other fund, and which has a small annual expense, and which is taxed, on sale, like a fund, so that long term gains in the fund are taxed at the long term capital gains rate, not at your income slab. Four, the gold systematic investment plan, called a gold SIP, which is where you buy a small amount of digital gold, or of the gold ETF, every month, and which is the most accessible, most low cost, most disciplined way to own gold, and which is the form I would recommend to most Indians, if they want gold in their portfolio.

When gold makes sense in a five asset portfolio. If your five asset portfolio is, broadly, equity, debt, gold, real estate, and cash, the role of gold is not to be the highest returner, and it is not, it is to be the diversifier, the asset that, in a bad equity year, often does not fall as much, or falls less, and that is tied to the global monetary system, in a way that Indian stocks are not tied. The sensible allocation of gold, in a balanced Indian portfolio, is in the range of five to ten percent of the total, not more, because the return is modest, and because it is a diversifier, not an engine. If you want more than ten percent, you are not diversifying, you are making a specific, and probably expensive, statement about the future, and you should know that you are doing that.

Fixed deposits, the laddering strategy, and the tax math. An FD, at an Indian bank, is a deposit, at a set rate, for a set term. The tax math is the part people get wrong. You do not pay tax at maturity. You pay tax on the interest, every year, as it is credited, at your income tax slab. So if you have a fixed deposit of ten lakh rupees, at seven percent, the interest is seventy thousand rupees a year, and that seventy thousand is added to your income, and taxed at your slab. If you are in the thirty percent slab, you are paying twenty one thousand rupees a year in tax on that interest, and your effective return, after tax, is five percent, not seven. That is the math that should drive your decision. If you are in the zero percent slab, the FD is fine, because you are not paying tax on the interest. If you are in a high slab, the FD is not as good as it looks, and a tax efficient instrument, like a direct bond held to maturity, or like PPF, may be better, because the tax treatment is cleaner, or because the interest is exempt.

The laddering strategy for FDs, the same idea as the bond ladder. Instead of one big FD at ten years, you put the money into five FDs, of two years each, staggered, so that every two years, a chunk matures, and you can reinvest it at the current rate, or take it out for a need, without breaking the whole deposit. The ladder protects you from locking a long, low rate, and it gives you a regular, predictable maturity, which is useful for planning.

The FD versus PPF versus bonds, for the conservative investor. If you are the truly conservative saver, the one for whom the idea of a bad equity year is genuinely frightening, the order of preference, usually, is this. First, PPF, for the tax free, safe, government backed, long term layer. Second, direct government bonds, on Retail Direct, for the safe, slightly higher yield, tax clean layer, held to maturity. Third, a ladder of FDs, for the flexible, liquid, very safe layer, the money you might need within a year or two. And fourth, if you have the temperament, a small slice in a very safe debt fund, for the truly short term parking, where you need the money in weeks or months. The conservative investor s portfolio is not boring, it is just quietly well structured, and it will, over time, build a corpus that is real, and tax efficient, and not dependent on any single instrument.

The digital gold platforms, what to check before you buy. One, the platform, is it a trusted name, a big bank, or a big broker s platform, or is it a small, unknown app, and if it is the small unknown app, why is it offering you a better price, and where does the money actually sit. Two, the spread, how much do you pay when you buy, and how much do you get back when you sell, because the spread is the real cost, and a wide spread eats your return. Three, the vault, where is the physical gold, and who is the custodian, and is that custodian a reputable, regulated vault, or is it a name you have never heard of. Four, the conversion, can you convert the digital gold into a physical coin or bar, at a reasonable cost, if you ever want to hold it in your hand, or is it locked in the digital form, forever. Five, the tax, how is the gold taxed, when you sell it, and is it treated like the physical gold you bought, or is it treated like a fund, and is the difference clear to you, before you buy.

## Chapter 20: Crypto, Alternative Assets, and the 2026 Frontier

This is the frontier chapter, the part where the rules are newer, the money is noisier, and the advice is, on purpose, the most conservative in the book.

The legal status of crypto in India, in plain terms. Crypto assets, in India, are legal to own, and legal to trade, but they are not legal tender, and they are not the currency, and they do not have the same legal footing as a rupee note or a bank deposit. The government has chosen, rather than banning them, to tax them, heavily, which is the current approach. You can buy, sell, and hold crypto, through a registered exchange, and you can do so within the tax rules that are described next. What you cannot do, comfortably, is treat crypto as a savings account, or as a pension plan, or as a place to put money you cannot afford to lose, because the tax treatment, and the volatility, and the legal status, all point in the same direction, which is that this is a speculative, high risk corner of the portfolio, and it should be run as one.

The thirty percent tax, the one percent TDS, and what they mean. The tax on crypto, in India, as of the current regime, works like this. When you sell a crypto asset for a profit, the profit is taxed at a flat thirty percent, and this is not added to your income in the same way as your salary, it is a separate, flat, high tax, and there is no deduction for the cost you paid, in the same clean way as with a normal asset, and there is no offsetting of a loss in one crypto against a gain in another, in the same way that you can offset a stock loss against a stock gain. So the tax, on a crypto gain, is a real, and heavy, drag. On top of that, when you sell crypto, and the sale is over a threshold, the exchange deducts, at the source, one percent, as Tax Deducted at Source, called TDS, which is money that is withheld, on the sale value, not on the profit, and which you can claim back, in part, when you file your return, if your final tax is lower than the amount withheld. The combined effect is that the tax friction on crypto, in India, is higher than on any other asset class you have read about in this book, and you should factor that friction into every decision, because it is not small, and it is not optional.

How to store crypto safely. The hot wallet is the wallet on your phone, or the wallet in the exchange s account, which is fast, and which is convenient, and which is where a lot of people keep a lot of their money, and which is also where a lot of people have had their money stolen, because the exchange was hacked, or because the account was compromised, or because a relative s login was phished. The cold wallet is the hardware wallet, the physical device that stores the keys, offline, which is slower to use, and which is the one you should use for any amount you genuinely care about, because the cold wallet is the closest thing to a locked safe, that the crypto world has. The paper wallet is the option, in between, which is where you write down the keys, on paper, and keep the paper in a safe, and which is a legitimate, if old school, storage method, for people who do not want to buy a hardware device. The rule is simple. Keep the hot wallet for small, daily, trade in and out amounts. Keep the cold wallet, or the paper wallet, for anything you intend to hold for a long time, and that you cannot afford to lose. And keep the recovery phrase, the set of words that unlocks the wallet, in two separate, physical places, and never in a note app on your phone, and never in a cloud backup, because the recovery phrase, in the cloud, is not a recovery phrase, it is a key that anyone who finds your cloud can use.

The small allocation rule, why two to five percent is the sane ceiling. The reason is not that crypto is bad, it is that the combination of the heavy tax, the extreme volatility, the regulatory uncertainty, and the real risk of a total loss on a single position, means that the asset class, even in its best years, is not a reliable wealth builder, for a person who is building wealth, and not speculating. If you put ten percent in, and it goes to zero, you have lost ten percent of your portfolio, and you need, in the equity and debt markets, a strong year just to get back to where you were, before you have even made a gain. If you put two to five percent in, and it goes to zero, the portfolio barely feels it, and you can, in a few good equity years, rebuild the slice, and move on. The two to five percent ceiling is not a moral judgment, it is a risk budget, and it is the risk budget that keeps the rest of the portfolio, the part that is actually building your life, intact.

The Digital Rupee, the e rupee, what it is, and whether it changes anything for you. The e rupee is the digital form of the rupee, issued by the Reserve Bank of India, called the RBI, which is the central bank, and which is the only entity that issues the currency, in any form, in India. The e rupee is not a crypto asset, and it is not a stablecoin, and it is not a new payment system in the way that the unified payment interface, called UPI, is a new payment system. It is the same rupee, in digital form, and it is legal tender, and it is backed by the same entity as the note in your wallet. Where it is used, as of writing, is in pilot programs, and in specific government payment schemes, and in a growing, but still small, number of retail payments, through the app, on a phone. Does it change anything for you, as a regular saver and investor, in the near term, not much. It is not a new asset to buy, it is not a new place to park money, and it is not a new interest earning instrument. It is the plumbing of the currency, and it is the plumbing that will, over time, make the way money moves, in India, cleaner, and faster, and more traceable. That is a good thing, and it is worth knowing, but it is not the kind of thing that changes your portfolio, today.

The other alternatives, REITs, infrastructure funds, and commodity funds. A Real Estate Investment Trust, called a REIT, is a company that owns real estate, mostly large commercial property, and that pays out a large share of its income, to the people who own the REIT, as a distribution. It is the way to own a slice of a large, well managed, commercial property portfolio, without buying a building, and without taking on the loan, and without dealing with the tenants. In India, there are a handful of listed REITs, and they pay a distribution yield that is often in the high single digit to low double digit range, and which is, on a yield basis, often better than a residential flat, and on a liquidity basis, better than any property, because you can sell the REIT units, on the stock market, any day. The infrastructure fund is a fund that invests in, or lends to, large infrastructure projects, roads, power, digital, and which pays a return that is in the range of the debt, plus a small equity premium. It is a middle road, between the safety of a bond, and the growth of a stock, and it is a reasonable part of a conservative to moderate portfolio. The commodity fund is a fund that tracks, or takes positions in, commodities, and it is a very different, and much more volatile, thing, and it is not a safe haven, it is a leveraged, and often a long only, bet on the price of a physical good, and it belongs, if it belongs at all, in the small, speculative corner, not in the core.

The protocol for when you see a two hundred times crypto pump. This is the one I want to make very clear, because it is the one that loses people the most. You see a coin, or a token, that has gone up two hundred times in a few weeks, and everyone you know is talking about it, and the app is flashing, and your friend made a fortune on it last year, and the fear of missing out, the FOMO, is loud. The protocol is this. One, do not buy, not today, and not this week, because the two hundred times move is already the move, and you are not early, you are late, and the people who are selling to you, right now, are the people who bought at one, and who are taking their profit, and who are, for the first time, not locked in, and who are free to sell, and who are, in most of these cases, going to sell, to you. Two, if you are going to buy, after you have slept on it, and after you have checked, and after you have written down, on paper, why you believe this, in one sentence, and that sentence is not that it went up two hundred times, buy only the two to five percent allocation, and not more, and not because you are excited, and not because the app is flashing, and not because your friend is telling you, buy because the allocation is the allocation, and the coin is the coin, and the decision is the decision, and the money is only what you can afford to lose, completely. Three, set a sell rule, before you buy, not after, and if the coin falls fifty percent from your entry, you sell, and you do not hope, and you do not average down, and you do not tell yourself that it is a long term hold, because a coin that has gone up two hundred times, and then falls fifty percent from your entry, is not a long term hold, it is a trade that has stopped being a trade, and it is now a loss, and a loss, on a speculative asset, is a loss, and it should be cut, and it should be cut cleanly, and it should be cut before it becomes the kind of loss that you keep adding to, because you cannot stand to be wrong. Four, if the coin goes up another two hundred times, and you still hold it, and you are still happy, that is fine, that is the upside, that is the reason you took the small slice, and that is why you did not put in ten percent, and that is why you did not put in your house.

That is the whole frontier, and the whole safe corner, and the whole government stack, and the whole boring, and the whole not so boring. The next part of the book, if you are still reading, and you have been reading, which you have, because you are here, the next part is about putting all of this together, the actual portfolio, the actual percentages, the actual numbers, and the actual, quiet, unglamorous, very Indian, very real, plan, that you can build, on a Tuesday evening, with a cup of tea, and a notebook, and a phone, and the rest of your life, in front of you.

---

## Part Four: Getting to Terms With the Taxman

This fourth part of the book is about the income tax, and we are going to treat it without fear and without flattery. The tax is not your enemy. It is a price you pay for living in India, and like most prices, you can negotiate it within the law. The chapters in this part cover everything a working earner needs to know about the two tax regimes, the deductions that legally reduce your bill, the taxes on your money as it makes more money, and the practical act of filing your income tax return so that you never sleep with one eye open waiting for a notice.

## Chapter 21 – Indian Income Tax: The Full Picture

Let us start with the big idea, because most Indians have a foggy picture of how their income is taxed. The income tax is charged on your income for the financial year, which runs from April 1 to March 31, and the bill is paid and filed the year after, which is called the assessment year. So income you earn in the financial year 2025 to 26 is reported and filed in the assessment year 2026 to 27. This one-year gap confuses every first time filer, so hold it tight.

The first thing to know is that India now has two sets of tax rules, and you must choose one of them when you file. These are called the old regime and the new regime, and from July 1, 2023, the new regime has been the default. The income tax department now pre selects the new regime when it processes your returns, so if you want the old regime, you have to say so clearly.

Think of the two regimes this way. The new regime is like a flat rate menu. Fewer discounts, but a lower base price. The old regime is like a discount driven menu. A higher base price, but if you buy the right things, you get many deductions and can bring the price way down. Which one you pick depends entirely on your personal situation, and we will walk through the decision tree in a moment.

### The New Regime in the Words of Your Salary

Let us tell the story of the new regime slabs for the financial year 2025 to 26, because these slabs changed on July 1, 2025, and the change is a genuinely good one for salaried Indians.

Imagine you are a salaried person, and your total taxable income under the new regime is, say, 7 lakh rupees. The first 4 lakhs of your income are taxed at zero percent. Yes, you read that right. The first 4 lakh rupees pay no income tax at all. Then the next chunk, from 4 lakhs up to 8 lakhs, is taxed at just 5 percent. The next chunk, from 8 to 12 lakhs, is taxed at 10 percent. From 12 to 16 lakhs, the rate is 15 percent. From 16 to 20 lakhs, it is 20 percent. And everything above 20 lakhs is taxed at 30 percent.

So in plain words, the new regime now looks like this: 0 percent up to 4 lakh, 5 percent from 4 to 8 lakh, 10 percent from 8 to 12 lakh, 15 percent from 12 to 16 lakh, 20 percent from 16 to 20 lakh, and 30 percent above 20 lakh. Before 2025, the zero tax slabs ended at 3 lakh rupees, and the lower slabs were much narrower, so the 2025 widening is the heart of the change. It means a person earning 12 lakh rupees now owes almost nothing at all, which is the whole point of what we will discuss next.

On top of the slab rates, there are two small add ons that matter only for high earners. If your income crosses 50 lakhs, a surcharge kicks in at 10 percent of your tax, rising to 15 percent above 1 crore, and 25 percent above 2 crore. And there is a cess, a small extra tax that funds health and education, which adds 4 percent to your total tax plus surcharge. If you are in the 30 percent slab and earn above 1 crore, your effective tax rate is closer to 31 percent once the cess is on.

### Section 87A: The Free Ticket for Earners Under 12 Lakhs

Here is the part of the new regime that made the 2025 budget very popular. Section 87A of the income tax act is a rebate, and a rebate is not a deduction. A deduction reduces your income before the tax is computed. A rebate reduces the tax itself, after it is computed. For the financial year 2025 to 26, if your taxable income under the new regime is up to 12 lakh rupees, you get a rebate of up to 60,000 rupees, which in effect wipes out the entire tax.

Let us do the arithmetic on 12 lakh, slowly. Under the new slabs, 4 lakhs pays zero. The next 4 lakhs at 5 percent is 20,000. The next 4 lakhs at 10 percent is 40,000. That adds up to 60,000 of tax on 12 lakh of income. And the Section 87A rebate is exactly 60,000. So a person whose taxable income is 12 lakh or less pays zero income tax under the new regime. No deductions, no savings in specific instruments, nothing. Zero. This is the single most important fact in this book for the large middle class of India.

Two conditions to keep in mind. First, the rebate is available to individuals only, not to companies, and it requires you to be a resident of India. Second, if your taxable income is even one rupee above 12 lakhs, the entire rebate disappears, and you owe the full slab tax. This cliff edge is real. It means a person earning 12 lakh and 1 rupee pays about 60,000 in tax, while the person next door earning 12 lakhs pays nothing. This is why you see people with slightly high incomes ask their accountant whether they can push a deduction into the old regime, and it is one of the classic break even points we will meet again.

### The Standard Deduction, Which Both Regimes Give You

The standard deduction is a deduction that needs no paperwork. It is for the cost of reaching and maintaining your job, and it simply reduces your taxable salary. For the financial year 2025 to 26, the standard deduction is 75,000 rupees for salaried individuals under both regimes, and for people aged 60 or more, it is 50,000. You do not have to prove anything. The company just deducts it when it calculates your tax. If you switch jobs during the year, the new employer continues it. It is one of the friendliest lines on the tax law, and most people never even think about it.

### Marginal Relief: The Brake on the Cliffs

Now here is a concept that protects you at every slab boundary. Suppose your income is just a little over a slab threshold. Say your taxable income is 4 lakh and 5 thousand. A naive calculation would tax the whole 4.5 lakhs, part at zero and part at 5 percent. But the law has a principle called marginal relief, and it says that your tax should not exceed the amount of income above the threshold by more than that amount.

Work it out. Your income above 4 lakhs is 5,000. The tax on that 5,000 at 5 percent is 250. Under marginal relief, your total tax cannot exceed 5,000, the amount above the threshold. Since 250 is well below 5,000, you pay 250. No problem. But now imagine your income is 1 crore and 10 thousand. The tax on that sliver is much bigger than 10 thousand. Marginal relief caps your tax at the amount above 1 crore, plus any surcharge on that. The idea is that crossing a threshold should not cost you more than the crossing itself. This is a quiet and powerful rule, and your tax software applies it automatically. You just need to know it exists so the number the software shows does not shock you.

### The Old Regime and the Deductions That Actually Matter

Under the old regime, the slab rates are higher. For the financial year 2025 to 26, the old slabs are: 0 percent up to 2.5 lakhs, 5 percent from 2.5 to 5 lakhs, 20 percent from 5 to 10 lakhs, and 30 percent above 10 lakhs, with the standard 75,000 deduction. Where the old regime wins is in the deductions. The three that matter most for most families are these.

The first is Section 80C, a basket of eligible investments where you can deduct up to 1.5 lakh. We will unpack the full list in the next chapter. The second is Section 80D, which lets you deduct health insurance premium, up to 25,000 for yourself and your family, plus up to 25,000 for your parents, and more for senior citizens. The third is Section 24(b), which lets a home loan holder deduct the interest paid, up to 2 lakhs for a self occupied house. These three, taken together, can shave 2.5 to 3.5 lakh of deductions off your income. At a 30 percent marginal rate, that is 75,000 to a lakh of tax saved.

The honest question is: do these deductions beat the lower new regime slabs? For a salaried person with about 2 lakh of 80C, 80D, and 24(b) combined, the new regime usually wins below around 22 to 24 lakh of gross income, and the old regime starts to win above that. There is no magic constant, because everyone s deductions are different, and the income tax department s calculator shows both answers side by side in 30 seconds. Use it. Do not guess. If your deductions are small, say under 75,000, the new regime is almost always better for you, and you owe the old regime nothing. If your deductions are big, the old regime deserves the math.

### The Five Step Method to Estimate Your Tax Before You File

Here is a method you can use every year, ideally in January, to know roughly what you owe without waiting for the income tax department to tell you.

1. Add up your total income. Your take home salary, plus bank interest, plus capital gains if you sold stocks or property, plus rental income, plus any business or freelance income. Round it. This is your gross income.
2. Subtract the standard deduction and any deductions your chosen regime allows. Under the new regime, mostly the standard deduction, and possibly the 50,000 National Pension System contribution and the home loan interest we will meet in the next chapter. Under the old regime, your full 80C, 80D, and 24(b) basket.
3. Apply the slab rates of your chosen regime to the result, and let the software or a simple calculator handle marginal relief at the cliff edges.
4. Add the surcharge if you earn above 50 lakhs, and add the 4 percent cess on top.
5. Subtract the tax already deducted at source, which is the tax your employer has already handed over. Find that number in Form 16 of your salary or in the Annual Information Statement, which is the online summary the tax department builds of every credit about you. If the credits exceed your tax, you get a refund. If they fall short, you owe the difference, and if the difference is 5,000 or more, that shortfall must be paid as advance tax, which brings us to the last part of this chapter.

### Advance Tax: The Four Installments

India taxes income as it is earned, and if you have income that no one deducts tax on, such as freelance work, capital gains, or interest, you must pay the tax yourself in installments, and this is called advance tax. It is not optional if your annual liability is 5,000 or more, and ignoring it costs you interest every quarter.

The rule is split into four installments on the tax you expect to owe for the whole year:

1. Fifteen percent by June 15.
2. Forty five percent by September 15.
3. Seventy five percent by December 15.
4. The full 100 percent by March 15.

You pay each installment on the income tax department website by the due date, and if you are under, the department charges interest. For a senior citizen whose only taxable income is interest, the first installment can be skipped entirely, and the whole advance is paid by March 15. A useful planning trick that many people do: in March, before the last installment, you can make a large National Pension System contribution under Section 80CCD(1B), up to 50,000, and book a fresh Section 80E education loan interest, both of which the new regime honors, to bring the final payment down. If you plan to file under the old regime, you can also top up your 80C basket in March. Note that if you paid too little advance tax, you face interest under Sections 234B and 234C, at one percent and half a percent per month respectively, and this interest has no cap. So estimate your advance tax each quarter with the five step method above, and top up installments rather than waiting for the bill.

## Chapter 22 – Deductions, Exemptions, and the Art of the Legal Tax Reduction

All tax planning in India is a game of reducing the income that is taxable, or of reducing the tax on that income, and the law gives you a rule book. In this chapter we walk through the rule book, section by section, and we tell you, honestly, which parts are strong, which are routine, and which have sharp edges.

Remember one framing idea first. There are two families of rules. Deductions from total income, like the 80C basket, which works under the old regime but not under the new, with a few exceptions we will name. And deductions from the tax itself, like the 87A rebate, which works under both regimes. Confusing these two is how people make planning errors.

### Section 80C: The One Point Five Lakh Basket

Section 80C is the famous basket, and it allows you to deduct from your taxable income the amount you invest or pay in the year, up to 1.5 lakh. The eligible instruments are:

1. Equity Linked Saving Scheme, or ELSS, a mutual fund with a 3 year lock in.
2. Public Provident Fund, or PPF, the long term government savings scheme.
3. Life insurance premium, paid on a policy for yourself, your spouse, or your children.
4. The principal repayment on a housing loan for a house you are buying or constructing.
5. Tuition fees for up to two children, at any school or university.
6. The Sukanya Kumar Fund, for a daughter born after 2015.
7. Senior Citizens Savings Scheme and certain National Savings Schemes, up to the basket limit.
8. The subscription or interest related investments in certain government instruments like the subscription to a public issue of securities.

One limit covers all of them. You can split 1.5 lakh any way you like across these instruments, but the total is capped. So 50,000 of PPF, 50,000 of ELSS, and 50,000 of tuition and insurance works perfectly. Two million of PPF works only to the extent of 1.5 lakh, and the rest earns interest but gives no deduction. ELSS is the popular choice inside the basket because it is a stock market fund, so it can beat inflation while locking the money for three years. But remember, you are buying equity risk to save 45,000 of tax at the 30 percent bracket, and that is a real trade off, not a free lunch. If you are in the zero tax zone under the new regime, the 80C basket does nothing for you, and you should simply invest in what serves your goals.

### Section 80D and 80DDB: Health Insurance and Real Illness

Section 80D deducts health insurance premium. For yourself, your spouse, and your dependent children, the limit is 25,000. For your parents, a separate 25,000. If either your parents or you are senior citizens, those personal limits rise to 50,000 each. A preventive health check up adds a tiny 500 on top, for the eligible period, but the bigger story is the family basket: in one good year, a family with senior parents can deduct up to a lakh of premium, saving 30,000 of tax at the top rate.

Section 80DDB is different, and more humanitarian. It deducts the actual medical expenditure you incur for a specified illness, up to 25,000, or 50,000 if you are a senior citizen, even if you do not have insurance. It is for conditions the government has listed, which includes most of the serious ones, like heart disease, stroke, kidney problems, and cancers, along with other chronic conditions. Keep the hospital bills, and the deduction is real. It is one of the most underrated lines in the law, because families in the hospital often do not even know it exists.

### Section 24(b): The Home Loan Interest, Two Lakh Limit

If you have a home loan on a house you live in, you can deduct the interest you pay in the year, up to 2 lakhs. The interest is not what you paid total, it is the part that is interest, and your bank sends you a certificate every year showing the exact split between principal and interest. Principal goes to 80C, interest goes to 24(b), and each has its own limit. A practical point: if you have pre paid a chunk of the loan, the interest drops, so do not rush prepayments if your goal is to keep the 24(b) deduction large. And if you rent out a house, the interest on that house s loan is deductible against the rental income, with no 2 lakh cap, though only against that income, not against your salary.

### Section 80E: The Education Loan, Unlimited and Long

Section 80E is a quiet gem. The interest you pay on an education loan, for yourself or for a child or even for a friend you guaranteed for you, is deductible with no cap, for up to 8 years. No limit, no basket, no paperwork beyond the interest certificate from the bank. And the important part for the new regime world: from the financial year 2025 to 26, the 80E interest deduction is allowed even under the new regime, which makes it one of the very few deductions that follow you no matter what you choose. If you took an education loan and are in a high bracket, this line can save you tens of thousands a year, and it runs for the full eight years, or stops earlier if the loan closes.

### Section 80CCD: NPS and the Pension Push

The National Pension System, or NPS, has its own deduction family. Under 80CCD(1B), you can deduct up to 50,000 of your own contribution to your NPS account, over and above 80C, so with a 1.5 lakh 80C you can save on a total of 2 lakh of pension and provident fund style investments. There is also 80CCD(2), which deducts up to 14,000 of what your employer contributes to your NPS, even if you have not opted for it. The employee part under 80CCD(1B) works in both regimes, so even a new regime filer gets the 50,000, which is why NPS is often the default answer to any Indian with a tax saving appetite. PPF lives inside 80C, as we saw, and the two work together.

### Section 80G: Donations and the Receipt Rule

If you donate to a registered charity, a temple trust with the right registration, a political party from the allowed list, or an approved fund, you get 80G. Some donations give 50 percent of the amount as a deduction, others give 100 percent, and a few have caps, like a percentage of your income. The rule that trips people up: you need a proper 80G receipt, either a physical one or the new electronic reference number, and you must have paid through banking, not cash, for amounts over 2,000. A nice amount of donation that you paid in cash and forgot to document saves you zero rupees. Plan the 80G at year end, get the receipts, and match the receipts to the year.

### The HUF Strategy, Told Honestly

HUF stands for Hindu Undivided Family, and it is the oldest tax planning trick in India. The idea is that a family, with a head and members, is a separate legal person in the tax law, and it gets its own set of slabs, and its own deductions. So a family can split its savings between individuals and the HUF, and in principle each box of income pays tax at its own low rate.

Now the honest part, because this is where the strategy gets over sold and where real problems live. First, the HUF is only for Hindus, Jains, Sikhs, and Buddhists, not everyone. Second, and this is the big one, the tax law has clubbing rules under Section 64 that say: income that is really your income, just routed through the HUF, is still taxed as your income. Money you earn as your salary and park in the HUF s bank account is your income, not the HUF s. If the HUF owns property that was gifted to it without real consideration, that gift can be taxed in your hands. If you transfer your assets to your spouse and claim they produce HUF income, the income comes back to you. The income tax department has, in its notices and its case law, made it clear that a HUF created only to save tax, with no genuine family property, no real family assets, and no real economic activity, will be looked through.

What is legitimate? The HUF having its own assets, like property inherited by the family before partition, or a genuine family business. The HUF receiving rental income on family property. The HUF having its own savings instrument in its name, and the HUF claiming deductions on that instrument. And that last point is the real power: a HUF can have its own 80C, its own 80D, and its own standard deduction of a kind, which doubles the family s total deduction capacity. Do it cleanly. Paper the family s creation of the HUF with a simple document, keep a separate bank account, keep separate records, and never use the HUF to park your own personal income. Ask a Chartered Accountant, a CA, to look at the setup once, because a sloppy HUF is exactly the kind of arrangement that draws a notice, and we will meet notices in Chapter 24.

### Section 80EEA: Home Loan Interest Under the New Regime

This is a newer and very specific line. Section 80EEA, spelled out fully, gives a deduction of up to 40,000 of the interest you pay on a home loan, even under the new regime, but only for a first time home buyer who took the loan between April 1, 2019 and March 31, 2025, and only for loans up to a certain ticket size, with the property bought up to a value limit. Note the number: the cap is 40,000, not 25,000. Many summaries get this wrong. If you are a first time buyer of a home in that window, this is real money. In a 30 percent bracket, it is worth up to 12,000 a year.

One sentence to close the chapter: the art of lawful tax reduction is not finding loopholes. It is knowing the list, picking the instruments that match your actual goals, and staying away from the aggressive structures the department is quietly watching.

## Chapter 23 – Capital Gains, Dividends, and the Tax on Your Money Making Money

So far, the tax was on your salary. This chapter is about the tax on your money, on the wealth you build. The law splits the gains by how long you held the asset, which creates the two big buckets, short term capital gains and long term capital gains, and each bucket has its own rate. We will also meet the special tax on dividends, the special tax on crypto, and the strategy of losses.

### Equity Gains: The 12 Month Rule, Fixed

For shares listed on a stock exchange, the rule is: if you hold a stock for more than 12 months, your gain on selling it is long term. If you hold it for 12 months or less, the gain is short term. It is not 18 months. It is 12. Get this one right in your head, because it decides which tax applies to your whole share portfolio.

The rates: short term equity gains are taxed at a flat 15 percent, no indexation, no exemption. Long term equity gains, the profits you make after holding for over 12 months, are taxed at 12.5 percent, but only on the portion of your annual long term gains above 1 lakh. The first 1 lakh of long term equity gains every year is completely tax free. This 1 lakh exemption has become the quiet workhorse of tax efficient investing in India. It is why a disciplined investor in a good fund, if their gains stay under 1 lakh a year, can compound for years with zero equity tax.

Two important refinements. First, there is a choice that most people never need: for stocks you bought before a certain date, the cost of acquisition can be taken as its market value on that date, not what you actually paid. Second, gains on unlisted shares and on some non equity fund types now carry a 12.5 percent long term rate, so the equity rules are not just for the exchanges.

### Debt Gains: Indexation, the 10 Percent Alternative, and Slab Rates

Debt is different from equity, and it has its own clocks. For bonds, debentures, and non equity mutual funds, long term means held for more than 36 months, three years. If you sell a bond after three years, the gain can be taxed two ways. The first way, and the one that is often better for older bonds, is 20 percent on the gain, but after adjusting the cost of the asset for inflation, a process called indexation. The second way is a flat 12.5 percent, the same as equity, on the raw gain, with no inflation adjustment. The taxpayer picks whichever is lower.

For debt mutual funds, indexation does not apply. Long term gains on debt funds are taxed at your own slab rate, same as your salary. And short term gains on any debt instrument are at your slab rate, full stop. So a top bracket investor holding a long bond can be in a better tax position than the same investor holding the same bond through a debt fund. This is one of the subtle reasons a Chartered Accountant is worth it for bond heavy portfolios.

A small but huge special case: the Sovereign Gold Bond, or SGB. If you hold it to maturity, which is 8 years, there is no capital gains tax at all, and the interest is tax deductible if you are in a high bracket. It is one of the cleanest instruments in the whole tax code.

### Property Gains and the Section 54 Reinvestment Rule

Land and building, held for more than 24 months, are long term assets, and a sale can generate a large gain. The Section 54 exemption lets you escape the tax on the gain if you use the sale amount to buy another residential property in the next two years, or to construct one in the next three years. You can even buy a property up to one year before the sale and count it. If you genuinely cannot buy, the law lets you park the amount in a Blocked Development Scheme, a specific government scheme, for up to six months, and the exemption holds. The classic trap: you buy the new house, sell it within three years, and the exemption is clawed back. So the reinvestment must be a real home, not a flip. The rule has other conditions, like the old house must have been held for a minimum period and the new one cannot be converted to cash quickly, and each has a fine print you must read with your CA before you close any sale.

### Crypto: Flat 30, No Indexation, No Losses

The rules on virtual digital assets, which is the formal name for crypto in the law, are deliberately unfriendly, and you should plan accordingly. The gain on selling crypto is taxed at a flat 30 percent, plus surcharge and cess, whether the holding is short or long term. There is no indexation, no 1 lakh exemption, and the worst part: you cannot set off a crypto loss against a crypto gain in the same year. If you lose on one coin and make on another, the loss cannot cancel the gain. The loss can be carried forward, but it can only be used to offset a future crypto gain, not your salary, not a stock gain, nothing. And any transfer of crypto over a threshold triggers a 1 percent Tax Collected at Source, a withholding called TCS, which the exchange deducts before you sell, even though it is not really tax, it is just a deposit.

The takeaway: hold crypto in a size that is a small, disposable fraction of your wealth, and treat every exit as a 30 percent taxable event. A person who cycles crypto in and out of a large position is, in effect, paying a 30 percent tax on the round trip. No one should be surprised by that, but most people are, until their CA shows them.

### Dividends: The 10 Percent at Source and the Old DDT

Before April 2020, dividends were taxed in a special way: the company paid a Dividend Distribution Tax, or DDT, out of its profits, and the shareholder got a tax free amount. That system ended. Now, dividends are ordinary income in your hands, taxed at your slab rate, and a 10 percent TDS is deducted at source by the company when it pays the dividend. If your taxable income is under 2.5 lakhs, you can even be exempt from dividend tax if you declare it, but that is a small case. The practical point: your dividend income shows up in your return as part of other income, at your slab rate, and the 10 percent TDS the company cut is your credit. If you are in the 30 percent bracket, dividends from a large company are not a tax efficient way to receive returns, and many investors now prefer capital gains through selling units, where the 12.5 percent long term rate applies, over dividends, which hit at 30 percent.

### Tax Loss Harvesting: Sell the Losers

Here is a strategy that is fully legal and very under used in India. The rule is that a capital loss can be set off against a capital gain in the same year, and a short term loss can be used against either a short or a long term gain, while a long term loss is only usable against a long term gain. What this buys you is a way to reduce the tax on your winners by first booking the losers.

The discipline: at the end of the financial year, in March, look at your portfolio for genuinely under performing positions, ones you are happy to sell anyway, and sell them before March 31. The loss is booked in the current year and can offset the gains you book that same year, and if the loss is bigger than the gain, the remaining loss is carried forward for eight years to offset future gains. And because you sold at a loss, when you later buy the same stock or fund, your new cost base is the current market price, so you have effectively reset the clock for the next gain. This is tax loss harvesting, and it is not aggressive at all, it is just using the law s own loss rules, which every investor s CA should remind you of once a year.

### FIFO and LIFO: The Average Cost Question

A common confusion: which purchase is deemed sold first, the first one, which is called First In First Out, or the last one, Last In First Out? In India, neither. The law uses the average cost of all units of the same asset that you held during the period. Every unit you bought of a particular stock or fund is pooled, the average cost per unit is computed, and that average is the cost that the gain is measured against. So your gain is computed on the average cost, not on a specific lot you picked. This is a cleaner, less manipulable rule than FIFO or LIFO, and your stock broker s statement will show it to you. The only place specific identity matters is the exception, where you hold units bought at different costs and there is a rule allowing specific identification only in narrow cases that your broker s software will handle for you.

One closing thought for the chapter: the tax on your money making money is not one number. It is a map, with 12 month lines for equity, 36 month lines for debt, 24 month lines for property, a special 30 percent flat for crypto, and your slab rate for dividends and debt funds. Learn the map once, and every portfolio decision gets easier.

## Chapter 24 – Filing the Income Tax Return, Avoiding Penalties, and Dealing with the Department

Now we come to the paperwork, and to the part of the year most people dread, the filing. I want to give you, first, the full list of documents, so you are never caught empty handed. Then, the step by step filing. Then, what a return actually is as a legal document, why refunds are slow, how to answer a notice, and when to bring in a professional.

### The Five Main ITR Forms, and When to Use Which

Income Tax Return, or ITR, comes in seven numbered forms, and the number tells the department what kind of income you have.

1. ITR 1, officially named Saurabh, is for individuals whose income is from salary, capital gains, one house property, and other sources that are not business. Most salaried people file ITR 1, and it is the simplest form.
2. ITR 2, officially named Sarvekshak, is for people who have the same incomes as ITR 1 plus capital gains in more detail, or who have business income declared on a specific method, but no direct business income. A lot of salaried investors with heavy capital gains file ITR 2.
3. ITR 3, officially named Sahaj, is for people who have business or professional income, computed on actuals, that is, not on the presumptive tax schemes. If you have a business, a freelance practice that is not presumptive, or you have depreciation on business assets, this is your form.
4. ITR 4, officially named Sugam, is for people on the presumptive taxation scheme, that is, you declare a fraction of your turnover as income without keeping full accounts, which is very popular for small professionals and small traders.
5. ITR 5 is for partnership firms and Limited Liability Partnerships, and for Associations of Persons and Bodies of Individuals, that is, groups that are not firms but act together.
6. ITR 6 is specifically for Associations of Persons and Bodies of Individuals that do not fit ITR 5.
7. ITR 7 is for trusts and religious and charitable institutions.

In plain terms: salaried plus capital gains plus one house is ITR 1 or ITR 2, business or professional income is ITR 3 or 4 depending on how you declare it, and everything else is 5, 6, or 7. If you are unsure which, the income tax department s website tells you which form applies based on your income types, and it takes one minute to pick.

### The Deadline, and the Extensions

The normal filing deadline for an individual, who is not audited, that is, whose accounts have not been checked by a Chartered Accountant for audit, is July 31. For audited cases, the department usually allows a later date, in recent years around December 31. Two practical things. First, for the financial year 2024 to 25, the government extended the individual deadline to August 15 of that year, as a one off extension, and such extensions are announced by a notification in the months before the due date, so check the official date, do not trust a calendar from last year. Second, if you miss the deadline, you can still file a belated return, up to December 31 of that year, but you lose the ability to claim a refund and you face a late filing fee, so do not use belated filing as a habit, use it as a last resort.

### The Document List, Before You Sit Down to File

Sit down with all of these, and the filing takes an hour instead of three days:

1. Your Permanent Account Number, or PAN, and your Aadhaar linked to it, both in front of you.
2. Form 16 Part A and Part B from your employer, the two parts of the salary certificate. Part A is the income, Part B is the tax deducted and paid.
3. Bank statements for every account you had interest in, and the interest certificates, in particular for savings, fixed deposits, and the interest on deposits over 10,000 a year.
4. The Annual Information Statement, or AIS, which the tax department builds for you from all the Tax Deducted at Source and Tax Collected at Source that was deducted on your PAN. Print it before you file, and read it, because it is the department s own view of your credits, and if you file against it, a mismatch is on you.
5. Dividend statements from your stock broker, and capital gain statements, in particular, the long term and short term split, because the form wants the split.
6. Mutual fund statements, that is, the capital gain statement your fund house sends in March, showing the gain and the TDS.
7. Home loan interest certificate from your bank, and for 80C, the PPF statement, the ELSS investment receipt, the life insurance premium receipts, and the education loan interest certificate.
8. For a property sold: the sale deed, the sale amount, the original cost, the date of purchase, and any 80G receipts you plan to claim.
9. The tax paid, that is, if you paid advance tax or self assessment, the challans, the payment receipts, which are now recorded in the AIS, but keep the references handy.
10. For a HUF: the HUF s PAN, the HUF s bank statement, and the documents showing the HUF s own assets and contributions.

If you keep these 10 items in a single folder every year, labeled by financial year, your filing day is calm and your life is easier.

### The ITR as a Legal Document: What It Proves and What It Does Not

This is a point that is misunderstood so much that I want to say it plainly. When you file your ITR, you are making a declaration to the government that you have reported your income and your tax, and that declaration is your word, recorded in a legal instrument. It does not, by itself, prove that you did not owe more. A return that is honest but incomplete is still a return, and the department can still open it later. It is also not, on its own, a document of your net worth, or a clean financial statement in the accounting sense, it is a tax return, and it is read by a tax official, not a banker, when it is offered. For a loan application, a bank wants a few more things, the last two years of ITRs, and usually the tax paid proof, and the bank s own ratio tests. So the ITR proves that you filed and that you computed the tax, and it is the foundation of your credit story, but it is not the whole story, and do not treat it as a shield from future scrutiny.

### Filing, Step by Step, in Plain Words

The process on the income tax department s own website, the one you reach through the e filing portal:

1. Register or log in with your PAN and your Aadhaar, or with a digital signature certificate if you have one. Most people use Aadhaar, and that is enough.
2. Choose the assessment year, the year you are filing for, and choose the form, ITR 1, 2, or whichever, and choose your regime, new or old, if the form asks.
3. The form pre fills a lot of data from your AIS and from the 26AS and from the employer s Form 16 that the department already has. Read every pre filled number. The pre fill is a start, not the truth, because it misses things like a new capital gain you just booked or a 80C investment you made after the last update.
4. Enter the income that is not pre filled: capital gains, other income, and your deductions, the 80C, 80D, 24(b), and so on, matching the receipts you have.
5. The form computes your tax, and then your credits from TDS, and the net is your refund or your balance to pay. If you owe, the form will not accept the filing until you pay, through the same portal, by net banking or debit card, and you get a challan that records the payment.
6. Verify the return, either by entering your 12 digit bank account number and the last six digits of your Aadhaar, that is, an electronic verification without a digital signature, or by sending the signed form to the department, or by using your digital signature. Electronic verification without a signature is the most common route today.
7. Download and keep the acknowledgement, that is, the page with the unique identification number of your filed return, and the ITR V, the verification form, if you used offline verification, which you send by post within 30 days. Keep both, forever, and keep the ITR of last year too, because when you file this year, you will be asked to confirm the number of your last year s return.

If at any step the form gives an error, read the error message, it is specific, and fix the line it names. Do not restart the whole form, do not panic, and do not guess, save and close, fix one line, reload.

### The Refund Trap, and How to Speed It Up

Most Indian refunds are late, and the reason is structural, not personal. A refund is only processed after the department has verified the ITR, and the department runs a verification queue, and in a year with 7 or 8 crore returns, the queue is long, and the money can sit in the account for months, sometimes into the next year. What you can do:

1. File early in the season, that is, in April and May, not on the last day, because early returns sit at the front of the queue.
2. Make sure your bank account details in the return match your bank s records exactly, because a mismatch in the account number is the single most common reason a refund bounces and restarts the clock.
3. Match the credits to your 26AS, and if there is a credit the return does not show, you are owed more, and you can claim it, and that claim is also what delays you, so be careful.
4. If you are owed a refund and the department owes you, interest is paid on refunds that are not made within the statutory period, at a modest rate, so the department is, technically, paying you for its delay.

And one structural note: the refund is not a new grant of money, it is the return of your own tax that was deducted too much, so plan your deductions so that the balance is close to zero, and if you do get a big refund, that is money that was locked in the government s account for a whole year with no interest you earned on it, so a big refund is usually a sign that you overpaid, which is a planning miss, not a windfall.

### The Notices, Section 143(1), 143(2), and the Reopening

A notice can arrive, and when it does, it can be any of these, and the right reaction depends on which one:

1. Section 143(1) is a low level, automated or semi automated notice, usually pointing to a mismatch between what the return said and what the AIS or the 26AS said, for example, you did not report a dividend that was actually paid to you, or you claimed a 80C against a receipt the system does not see. You respond on the same e filing portal, usually within 15 or 30 days, with a clear explanation and, where possible, the document, and you close the loop.
2. Section 143(2) is a full assessment notice, that is, a real officer is going to verify the return line by line. This is more serious. You must respond in writing, with the document trail, and you are within your rights to a fair, written, reasoned process, and if you disagree with the outcome, you have a formal appeal process, to the Income Tax Appellate Tribunal and beyond.
3. Sections 147 and 148 are the reopening powers, that is, the department can, within a set time window, reopen a year it has already processed if it believes income has escaped assessment. This is the power most people fear, and it is real, but it is bound by time limits and by the requirement that the officer had a reason, and a reopening without a real reason is challengeable.

Two honest pieces of advice, that will save you from 80 percent of the fear. First, if your return is honest and your documents are clean, a 143(1) or 143(2) is usually a paperwork problem, not a fraud finding, and the paperwork is almost always fixable. Second, if you get a 147 or 148, do not fight it in the moment, and do not sign anything you do not understand, and get a Chartered Accountant into the room, because a reopening is a formal legal process, and the stakes and the time limits are real.

### The Penalty Structure, Correctly

A very common piece of folklore is that there is a late filing penalty of one percent per month, capped at 50,000, and I want to correct the record, because this belief has cost people the right planning. The actual structure is:

1. Section 234F is the late filing fee, a fixed amount, not a percentage: 500 rupees if your income is up to 5 lakhs, 1,500 if it is above 5 lakhs, and it applies when you miss the deadline, even if you do not owe tax.
2. Section 234A is interest on tax that you did not pay by the due date of assessment, at one percent per month or part of a month, and it has no cap, it runs until you pay.
3. Section 234B is interest on advance tax that was not paid, at one percent per month on the shortfall.
4. Section 234C is interest on advance tax that was paid late, that is, paid in the wrong installment, at half a percent per month on the shortfall for that installment.
5. Section 270A is a 10 percent penalty on under reported income, that is, income the department believes you declared less than it actually was, and this is the one that is genuinely punitive, and it is the one that a clean, well documented return protects you from.

So if you are late, the cost is the 500 or 1,500 fee plus the interest, and the interest is the real cost, because it runs every month, without a cap, until you settle. If you owe advance tax, the 234B and 234C interest is on you, and it is monthly, and it is not capped. Plan your advance tax on time, and the interest does not get a chance.

### When to File Yourself, and When to Call a Chartered Accountant

The honest rule is: if your income is salary plus a small, clean investment return, and you file the same simple ITR 1 or ITR 2 year after year, you can absolutely file yourself, and you should, because the experience of doing it once a year is valuable, and the fees for a simple return are modest, and doing it yourself costs nothing but an evening. You need a professional when: your income includes business, you have a 143(2) or 147 notice, you have a HUF that is doing real planning, you have cross border income or assets, you have a crypto position that is large enough to matter, you sold property, or you have more than one source of income in a year. And there is one rule of thumb that I close on: if the professional fee you would pay is less than 10 percent of the tax you stand to save by getting the return right, hire the professional, every time. The fee buys you a clean file, and a clean file is worth more than the fee.

And to close this part of the book, one sentence that covers it all: the income tax is not a mystery to be feared, it is a rule book to be read, and you have now read the whole rule book, and the only thing left to do is to file, honestly, on time, every year, and the rest of your financial life gets calmer for it.

---

# Part 5: Protecting What You Have, and Planning What Comes Next

In this part we move from making money to protecting it and turning it into security. We start with the policy that quietly decides whether a family survives a hospitalisation, and we finish with the most exciting number in personal finance, your financial independence number. Between those two we cover life insurance, wills and estates, the duty and the math of caring for ageing parents, the full retirement picture, and the safety net underneath it all: the social security systems the government built for working Indians.

## Chapter 25: Health Insurance in India: The Most Important Policy You Own

Let us start with a difficult truth. In most Indian families, a health policy matters more than a life policy. A life policy protects your family only after you die, and never pays out while you are alive and suffering. But one major hospitalisation, a heart bypass, a cancer treatment, a long hospital stay, a delivery complication, can quietly cost three to ten lakh rupees and wipe out ten years of savings before dinner is on the table. Health insurance is the one policy that pays out while you are still here, still earning, still trying to hold the family together. That is why, for most working Indians under sixty, we treat health cover as the first protection, ahead of everything else.

### Why health insurance beats life insurance for most Indians

A middle class family of four can afford a good term life policy for just a few thousand rupees a year, and that policy replaces your income if you die. But the risk that actually lands on your head while you are alive is illness. Illness does two things at once. It removes your income while you recover, and it creates a large new expense. A life policy helps with neither. A health policy helps with the expense, and that is the core reason we put health cover first.

There is a second reason. Hospital costs in India have kept rising faster than general inflation, so a policy you bought when you were twenty five is far more useful now, and the real danger is not that the policy is worthless, but that the sum insured has become too small. We will return to that soon.

### The five main types of health insurance

Walk into any insurance desk and you will hear these names. Let us demystify them one at a time.

1. An individual health policy. It covers exactly one person. It is clean, but if you are married with children, buying one for yourself alone rarely makes sense, because the point is to protect the whole household.
2. A floater policy. One sum insured, a single pot, floats among a small group, usually a spouse and a child or two. When one person claims, everyone shares the limit, and it is cheaper than separate individual policies.
3. A family floater policy, the most popular product in India. One sum insured covers you, your spouse, and typically up to two or three children under one policy, the natural first choice for a young family. The catch is that all members share the same pot, so if two people are hospitalised in a year, the pot shrinks fast.
4. A senior citizen health policy. These are built for people usually over sixty, with higher premiums, smaller covers, longer waiting periods, and more exclusions. You buy one when the standard family policy will not cover an ageing parent.
5. A top up policy, the smartest trick in this chapter. It is a policy that pays only after your main policy runs out, and we will explain it at the end.

### The sum insured question: how much cover you really need

This is the question that separates people who buy a policy from people who are actually protected. A common mistake is to buy one because it is cheap, and the ease of a three or five lakh family floater hides the problem. In a Tier 1 city in 2026, a serious hospitalisation with intensive care, a cardiac procedure, or cancer, can quietly cross seven or eight lakh rupees. If your cover is five lakh, the policy pays its full limit and your family pays the rest. So treat five lakh rupees as the absolute minimum for a family in any city, the price of admission, and not enough. Treat ten lakh as the target for a family of four, and ten to fifteen lakh if you live in Delhi, Mumbai, Bangalore, or Chennai, where hospital bills are heaviest in the country.

The cleanest way to think about it is this. Take the average cost of a serious hospitalisation in your city, add the hospitalisation costs of your two most important people, and add a buffer for rising costs. If that lands at eight lakh, buy at least ten. If it lands at twelve, buy fifteen.
